On stability of self-similar blowup for mass supercritical NLS
Zexing Li
Address: Laboratoire AGM
CY Cergy Paris Université
2 avenue Adolphe Chauvin
95300 Pontoise
France
Email address: zexing.li@u-cergy.fr
Abstract.
We consider the mass supercritical (NLS) i\partial_{t}u+\Delta u+u|u|^{p-1}=0 in dimension d\geq 1 in the mass-supercritical range 0<s_{c}=\frac{d}{2}-\frac{2}{p-1}<1 . The existence of self-similar blow up dyamics is known [51], and suitable self-similar blow up profiles were constructed in [2]. In this work, we establish the finite codimensional nonlinear asymptotic stability in homogeneous Sobolev space and energy space H^{1} for a large class of self-similar profiles. The heart of the proof is, following the approach of Beceanu [4], the derivation of Strichartz dispersive estimates for matrix operators of the form
\mathcal{H}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{b}-1&\\
&-\Delta_{-b}+1\end{array}\right)-ibs_{c}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{1}&W_{2}\\
-\overline{W_{2}}&-W_{1}\end{array}\right),
b>0 and
\Delta_{b}=\Delta+ib\left(\frac{d}{2}+x\cdot\nabla\right),
inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces. The deformed Laplacian \Delta_{b} arises from the renormalization and its operator group e^{it\Delta_{b}} exhibits self-similar dispersion, which not only recovers the free Strichartz but also enables an extension of resolvent families. Compared with Strichartz estimates based on \Delta , this one has a larger admissible region, works for arbitrarily small polynomial decaying potential, and requires no spectral assumption.
1. Introduction
1.1. Setting of the problem
We consider the nonlinear Schrödinger equation
\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}i\partial_{t}u+\Delta u+u|u|^{p-1}=0\\
u_{|t=0}=u_{0}\end{array}\right.
(NLS)
for spatial dimension d\geq 1 and p>1 . The scaling symmetry
u_{\lambda}(t,x):=\lambda^{\frac{2}{p-1}}u(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x),\quad\lambda>0
allows us to compute the critical Sobolev norm invariant
\|u_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{c}}}=\|u(\lambda^{2}t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{c}}},\quad s_{c}:=\frac{d}{2}-\frac{2}{p-1}.
In this paper, we are interested in describing singularity formation mechanisms for nonlinearities above the mass and below the energy conservation laws:
0<s_{c}<1.
1.2. Type I and Type II blowup.
We now recall some key facts related to the description of singularity formation for (NLS) with 0<s_{c}<1 .
The self-similar law. From an elementary scaling argument, all blow up solutions satisfy the scaling self-similar lower bound
\|u(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\gtrsim(T-t)^{-\frac{\sigma-s_{c}}{2}}
(1.1)
where s_{c}<\sigma\leq 1 is supercritical. We say a solution blows up self-similarily if it saturates the self-similar law
\|u(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\sim(T-t)^{-\frac{\sigma-s_{c}}{2}},\quad s_{c}<\sigma\leq 1.
(1.2)
Type II blow. Type II solutions which blow up strictly faster than (1.2) have been constructed both in the critical case s_{c}=0 , [53, 44, 46, 42, 43, 45, 54]), and the supercritical case through the derivation of "ring" solutions which concentrate on a circle, [22, 55, 52, 29].
Type I blow. However, for 0<s_{c}<1 , type I blow is conjectured to exist and generate stable blow up dynamics, [63, 62]. More precisely, the self-similar renormalization
u(t,x)=\frac{e^{i\gamma(t)}}{(\lambda(t))^{\frac{2}{p-1}}}v\left(\tau,y\right)
with the explicit laws
\lambda(t)=\sqrt{2b(T-t)},\quad\gamma(t)=\tau(t),\ \ \tau(t)=-\frac{1}{2b}\ln(T-t),\ \ y=\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}
(1.3)
maps (NLS) onto the renormalized flow
i\partial_{\tau}v+\Delta v-v+\frac{ib}{2}\left(\frac{2}{p-1}v+y\cdot\nabla v\right)+v|v|^{p-1}=0.
(1.4)
In the range 0<s_{c}<1 , admissible self-similar profiles are expected to exist from numerical grounds, [41, 36, 35, 62, 6, 5].
Conjecture (Existence of suitable profiles). Let d\geq 1 and s_{c}\in(0,1) . Then there exists b=b(d,s_{c})>0 and a smooth radially symmetric profile Q_{b} with the following properties.11
1
The equation (1.5) and self-similar decay (1.7) are essential, in that they can imply the non-vanishing property (1.6) and self-similar decay for higher order derivatives (1.8) via ODE analysis, see [66, Lemma 2.2] and [37] respectively. We remark that the main result of [66] was unfortunately dubious.
(i)
Equation: Q_{b} is a stationary solution to (1.4):
\Delta Q_{b}-Q_{b}+ib\left(\frac{2}{p-1}Q_{b}+y\cdot\nabla Q_{b}\right)+Q_{b}|Q_{b}|^{p-1}=0.
(1.5)
(ii)
Non-vanishing:
Q_{b}(x)\neq 0\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
(1.6)
(iii)
Self-similar decay:
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}_{r\to\infty}r^{\frac{2}{p-1}}|Q_{b}(r)|=c_{p}>0,
\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}|Q_{b}^{\prime}(r)|<\infty;
(1.7)
\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{\frac{2}{p-1}+k}|\partial_{r}^{k}Q_{b}^{\prime}(r)|<\infty,
\displaystyle\forall\,\,k\geq 2.
(1.8)
The existence of such profiles has been obtained by Bahri-Martel-Raphaël [2] in the range 0<s_{c}\ll 1 by bifurcating from the critical case s_{c}=0 , see also [33] for the case of (gKdV). Extending the branch to higher values of s_{c} is a delicate nonlinear ODE problem, and recently Donninger-Shörkhuber [17] constructed the suitable profile for the physical scenario d=p=3 with computer assistance. Let us stress that the construction of self-similar profiles is central in the study of singularity formation mechanisms and has been mostly addressed in the energy supercritical case s_{c}>1 , see [12] for the heat equation, [13] for the wave equation and [28, 50] for compressible fluids.
Existence of self-similar dynamics. Self-similar profiles satisfying (1.7) are not in the energy space H^{1} and hence cannot be used as such to produce finite energy type I solutions. The existence of such dynamics was first obtained in [51] for 0<s_{c}\ll 1 using again a bifurcation argument from the critical case s_{c}=0 . The analysis provides solutions that satisfy the self-similar law (1.1), but does not describe the asymptotic structure of the singularity which should be given by the self-similar profile constructed in [2].
1.3. Main results
Our aim in this paper is to provide a robust approach for the proof of the asymptotic stability of self-similar profiles.
1.3.1. \dot{H}^{s_{c}+} stability theory
Theorem 1.1 (Finite-codimensional asymptotic stability in \dot{H}^{s_{c}+} ).
Let d\geq 1 , s_{c}\in(0,1) , and b,Q_{b} satisfying (1.5), (1.6), (1.7). Then for any 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 , there exists a Lipschitz finite-codimensional manifold \mathcal{M}\subset\dot{H}^{\sigma} of initial data
u_{0}=Q_{b}+\varepsilon_{0}
where \|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\ll 1 , such that the corresponding solution to (NLS) blows up at T=\frac{1}{2b} with a decomposition
u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{\frac{2}{p-1}}}(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)\left(t,\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}\right)e^{i\tau(t)}
(1.9)
where (\lambda(t),\gamma(t),\tau(t)) are given by (1.3) and
\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{x}}\lesssim(T-t)^{\frac{\sigma-s_{c}}{2}}.
(1.10)
Moreover, there exists u_{*}\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} such that
u(t)-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{\frac{2}{p-1}}}Q_{b}\left(\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}\right)e^{i\tau(t)}\to u_{*}\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\dot{H}^{\sigma}\quad as\quad t\to T.
(1.11)
The result reduces the nonlinear asymptotic stability to a linear spectral stability problem. As a corollary, assuming the unstable directions of the linearized operator are generated merely by symmetries of the equation (mode stability), we can obtain the asymptotic stability without losing codimensions.
Assumption 1.2 (Mode stability).
Let d\geq 1,s_{c}\in(0,1) , and b,Q_{b} satisfying (1.5), (1.6), (1.7). Let {\mathcal{H}} be the linearized operator around Q_{b} in self-similar coordinates defined by (1.4)-(1.26). For any 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 , the discrete spectrum of {\mathcal{H}} satisfies
\sigma_{\rm disc}\left({\mathcal{H}}\big|_{(\dot{H}^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{2}}\right)\cap\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Im z<b(\sigma-s_{c})\right\}=\{0,-bi,-2bi\},
(1.12)
with the corresponding Riesz projections satisfying22
2
They are instabilities generated by phase rotation, spatial translation and scaling symmetry respectively. See Section 6.3 for detailed discussion.
\dim{\rm Ran}P_{0}=1,\quad\dim{\rm Ran}P_{-bi}=d,\quad\dim{\rm Ran}P_{-2bi}=1.
(1.13)
Remark 1.3 (Verification of mode stability).
Proving Assumption 1.2 for certain self-similar profile is an independent linear problem. It requires an exact characterization of unstable spectrum for a non-self-adjoint operator, which is relatively unbounded with respect to (matrix) Schrödinger operators. In our forthcoming work [37], we show this property holds for the slightly mass-supercritical profiles constructed in [2] for d\leq 10 , as expected from the stability result [51].
Theorem 1.4 (Conditional asymptotical stability in \dot{H}^{s_{c}+} ).
Under the same assumption of Theorem 1.1 plus mode stability Assumption 1.2, for any 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 , there exists \epsilon_{1}\ll 1 such that for all \|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}<\epsilon_{1} , there uniquely exists (\lambda_{0},x_{0},\theta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R} with
\displaystyle|\lambda_{0}-1|+|x_{0}|+|\theta_{0}|\lesssim\|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
such that the initial data u_{0}=Q_{b}+\varepsilon_{0}
generates a solution blowing up at T=\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2b} satisfying the decomposition
u(t,x)=\frac{1}{(1-2b\lambda_{0}^{-2}t)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}}(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)\left(t,\frac{x-x_{0}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-2bt}}\right)e^{-i\left[\frac{\ln(\frac{1}{2b}-\lambda_{0}^{-2}t)}{2b}+\theta_{0}\right]}
(1.14)
with the decaying of perturbation (1.10) and the existence of blowup profile in \dot{H}^{\sigma} (1.11).
Comments on Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.4.
1. Regularity. (NLS) is local-wellposed in \dot{H}^{\sigma} with 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 , see Proposition D.1, so the evolution makes sense with merely u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} . This result is almost sharp in terms of regularity for \dot{H}^{s} spaces, and the critical \dot{H}^{s_{c}} stability is still open.
2. Manifold structure of Theorem 1.1. Let B_{\epsilon_{0}}^{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}:=\{f\in\dot{H}^{\sigma},\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}<\epsilon_{0}\} with \epsilon_{0}\ll 1 , then we construct a direct sum decomposition
\dot{H}^{\sigma}=X_{u}\oplus X_{cs}
(1.15)
with X_{cs} and X_{u} are \mathbb{R} -linear subspaces and X_{u} finite-dimensional, and a Lipschitz map \Phi:B_{\epsilon_{0}}^{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\cap X_{cs}\to X_{u} with \Phi(0)=0 , such that the manifold in Theorem 1.1 can be realized as
\mathcal{M}=({\rm Id}+\Phi)(B_{\epsilon_{0}}^{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\cap X_{cs})+Q_{b}.
(1.16)
Moreover, this manifold is tangential to the center-stable hyperplane B_{\epsilon_{0}}^{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\cap X_{cs}+Q_{b} . See Theorem 6.4 for more accurate formulation.
1.3.2. H^{1} stability theory
The self-similar decay (1.7) makes Q_{b}\notin\dot{H}^{s} of any s\in[0,s_{c}] , which raises the question for the existence, stability and behavior of Type I blowup in the energy space H^{1} .33
3
Nevertheless, the existence of H^{1} open set of data leading to Type I blowup with vanishing perturbation (1.10) is included in the result Theorem 1.4 assuming Assumption 1.2. Based on the proof of \dot{H}^{\sigma} stability, we also prove the counterpart of Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.4 in H^{1} .
Theorem 1.5 (Asymptotic stability in H^{1} ).
Let d\geq 1 , s_{c}\in(0,1) , and b,Q_{b} satisfying (1.5), (1.6), (1.7), (1.8).
(i)
Finite-codimensional asymptotic stability:
For R_{1}\gg 1 , there exists a Lipschitz finite-codimensional manifold \mathcal{M}\subset H^{1} of initial data
such that the corresponding solution to (NLS) blows up at T=\frac{1}{2b} with the decomposition (1.9) for (\lambda(t),\gamma(t),\tau(t)) are given by (1.3) and decaying of perturbation
\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1}}\lesssim(T-t)^{\frac{\sigma-s_{c}}{2}}
(1.17)
where 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 .
Moreover, there exists u_{*}\in\dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1} and u^{*}\in H^{\tilde{\sigma}} for every 0\leq\tilde{\sigma}<s_{c} such that
\displaystyle u(t)-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{\frac{2}{p-1}}}Q_{b}\left(\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}\right)e^{i\tau(t)}\to u_{*}
\displaystyle\mathrm{in}\,\,\dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1}\quad as\,\,t\to T,
(1.18)
\displaystyle u(t)\to u^{*}
\displaystyle\mathrm{in}\,\,H^{\tilde{\sigma}}\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,t\to T.
(1.19)
and with p_{c}=\frac{2d}{d-2s_{c}} , we can quantify the critical norm blowup as t\to T
\|u(t)\|_{L^{p_{c}}}\sim|{\rm log}(T-t)|^{\frac{1}{p_{c}}}(1+o(1)),\quad\|u(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{c}}}\sim|{\rm log}(T-t)|^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+o(1)).
(1.20)
(ii)
Conditional asymptotic stability: If we further assume Assumption 1.2, then there exists an open set of initial data {\mathcal{O}}\subset H^{1} such that its solution to (NLS) blows up at T=\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2b} and satisfies the decomposition 1.14 with |(\lambda_{0},x_{0},\theta_{0})|\ll 1 . For some 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 , the properties (1.17)-(1.20) remain true.
Comments on Theorem 1.5.
1. Comparison with stability result [51].
Combined with Remark 1.3, this completes [51] by deriving the asymptotic stability of the self-similar profile and provides a different route map towards the stability. Besides, since our argument is independent of energy structure, it should be natural to generalize to higher regularity H^{k} .
2. Structure of manifold and open set. Here we use a different subspace decomposition for H^{1} as
H^{1}=\tilde{X}_{cs}\oplus\tilde{X}_{u},
with \tilde{X}_{u}\subset C^{\infty}_{c} and being finite-dimensional. Denote \chi_{R_{1}} to be a smooth cutoff as (1.31) and B^{H^{1}}_{\epsilon_{1}} be the \epsilon_{1} -ball in H^{1} . Given an R_{1}\gg 1 , the manifold {\mathcal{M}} in (i) and open set {\mathcal{O}} in (ii) are constructed as
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle Q_{b}\chi_{R_{1}}+\varepsilon^{*}_{R_{1}}+B^{H^{1}}_{\epsilon_{1}}
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle Q_{b}\chi_{R_{1}}+\varepsilon^{*}_{R_{1}}+({\rm Id}+\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{1}})(B^{H^{1}}_{\epsilon_{1}}\cap\tilde{X}_{cs})
where \varepsilon^{*}_{R_{1}}\in\tilde{X}_{u}\subset C^{\infty}_{c} with \|\varepsilon^{*}_{R_{1}}\|_{H^{1}}=o_{R_{1}\to\infty}(1) , and \tilde{\Phi}_{R_{1}}:B^{H^{1}}_{\epsilon_{0}}\cap\tilde{X}_{cs}\to\tilde{X}_{u} , \tilde{\Phi}_{R_{1}}(0)=0 is a Lipschitz map. See Section 7.3 for detailed description.
3. Blowup of the critical norm.
The asymptotics of critical norm (1.20) answers a question in [47] for the expectation of sharp power low of its logarithmic divergence. It also indicates that subcritical limit profile u^{*}\notin\dot{H}^{s_{c}} as stated in the stability result [51].
1.4. Strichartz estimates for the self-similar propagator
Let the deformed Laplacian be
\displaystyle\Delta_{b}:=\Delta+ib\left(\frac{d}{2}+y\cdot\nabla\right).
Structure of the linearized operator. The linearization of the time-dependent flow (1.4) close to a self-similar solution Q_{b} solution to (1.5) leads to a non-self-adjoint linearized problem of the form
i\partial_{\tau}Z+{\mathcal{H}}Z=F(Z).
(1.21)
Here F is the nonlinear term, Z=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Z_{1}\\
Z_{2}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2} and
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{b}-1&\\
&-\Delta_{-b}+1\end{array}\right)-ibs_{c}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{1}&W_{2}\\
-\overline{W}_{2}&-W_{1}\end{array}\right)
for the explicit potentials
\displaystyle W_{1}=\frac{p+1}{2}|Q_{b}|^{p-1},
\displaystyle\quad W_{2}=\frac{p-1}{2}Q_{b}^{2}|Q_{b}|^{p-3}
(1.26)
which under (1.7) satisfy a polynomial decay
|W_{i}(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\alpha},\quad|\nabla W_{i}(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\alpha-1}
(1.27)
for i=1,2 for some \alpha>0 .
Decay for the linearized operator. Various strategies have been used in the literature to address the derivation of local-in-space decay estimates for linearized operators close to self-similar profiles: local Virial estimates [39, 44, 48], self-adjoint spectral estimates [12], distorted Fourier transform [34], semigroup estimates [16, 49, 32].
Strichartz estimates were derived in the pioneering work [14] to control the flow around self-similar blowup of spherically symmetric 3D energy-critical nonlinear wave equations in the renormalized light cone.
Strichartz bound. Our second main result in this paper is the derivation of global in space Strichartz estimates which can be directly used for the proof of the nonlinear stability Theorem 1.1. One edge of Strichartz estimate here, compared with semigroup method, is that it requires at most one derivative, hence works nicely for weak and non-analytic nonlinearities.
Our approach follows the framework developed by Beceanu for the derivation of Strichartz in the b=0 case.
Theorem 1.6 (Strichartz estimates).
Let d\geq 1 , b>0 and s_{c}\in(0,1) . Let the linear matrix Schrödinger system,
i\partial_{\tau}Z+\mathcal{H}Z=F
(1.28)
with \mathcal{H} as in (1.4), W_{1} and W_{2} satisfying (1.27) for some \alpha>0 . Then there exists \delta_{1}>0 such that for all \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) , there exists a finite rank projection operator P_{\rm disc}:\dot{H}^{\sigma}\to\dot{H}^{\sigma} such that the following holds. Let \nu\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) and P_{\rm ess}:={\rm Id}-P_{\rm disc} ,
then
\displaystyle\|e^{\nu t}P_{\rm ess}Z\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{\tau}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}}_{x}}\lesssim\|P_{\rm ess}Z(0)\|_{\dot{H^{\sigma}}}+\|e^{\nu t}P_{\rm ess}F\|_{L^{q_{2}^{\prime}}_{\tau}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}_{x}}
(1.29)
where (q_{i},p_{i}) for i=1,2 satisfies
\displaystyle(q,p,d)\neq(2,\infty,d),\quad(q,p)\in\left\{q\geq 2,2<p\leq\infty,\frac{2}{q}+\frac{d}{p}\geq\frac{d}{2}\right\}\cup\{(\infty,2)\}.
(1.30)
Comments on Theorem 1.6.
1. Deformed Laplacian \Delta_{b} .
Compared with the standard Laplacian \Delta , the correction part ib(\frac{d}{2}+y\cdot\nabla) is not relatively bounded and changes the spectrum drastically to be \sigma(\Delta_{b})=\mathbb{R} with b\neq 0 (see Lemma 2.5).
Current results for variable-coefficient Schrödinger operators (for example [7, 61, 40]) do not directly apply since \Delta_{b} is no longer asymptotically flat.
Fortunately, the semigroup e^{it\Delta_{b}} enjoys good structure and has explicit representation (2.2)-(2.3) (first derived by Carles in [9, 10]). Here we list two main features:
(1)
\dot{H}^{\sigma} -energy decay. The commutator of \Delta_{b} with fractional differentiation D^{\sigma} gives constant ib\sigma , leading to an exponential decay e^{b\sigma t} of e^{it\Delta_{b}} in \dot{H}^{\sigma} as indicated in (1.29).
(2)
Self-similar dispersion. The self-similar rescaling in time makes e^{it\Delta_{b}} exhibit exponential dispersive decay as t\rightarrow\infty . As an important corollary, the resolvent families can be extended onto and even beyond the real axis.
2. Comparison with Donninger’s work [14].
In [14], Donninger first represents the semigroup e^{t\mathbf{L}} by resolvents (\lambda-\mathbf{L})^{-1} via Laplace transform, and further solves for Green’s functions to represent the resolvents. Then e^{t\mathbf{L}} is a sum of free part and perturbation given by potential, and Strichartz estimates of each part follow intricate spatial estimates. This explicit construction is very clear in treating the radial case.
In this work, we exploit nice structure of the free operator group e^{it\Delta_{b}} and deal with the potential with a more abstract framework invented by Beceanu [4], which utilizes the convolution structure of the semigroup and resolvent identities to reduce the Strichartz estimates to a uniform invertibility problem. The semigroup e^{it{\mathcal{H}}} is decomposed into a composition of the free part and the potentials (Birman-Schwinger principle) rather than a summation. This strategy avoids explicitly solving resolvent equations and thus can handle non-radial case in the whole space.
3. Comparison with Strichartz estimates for Schrödinger equation with potentials.
There have been many works devoting to Strichartz estimates of e^{it(-\Delta+W)} or its matrix version, arising from Schrödinger equation with potentials or linearization around solitons [14, 30, 59, 8, 25]. Since we replace the Laplace with a deformed one \Delta_{b} , our Strichartz estimates have the following new features:
(1)
Enlarged admissible region of (q,p) . The self-similar dispersive rate at infinity implies a larger admissible region (1.30) (Figure 1) for e^{it\Delta_{b}} (2.6) and ultimately for e^{it{\mathcal{H}}} , instead of just \frac{2}{q}+\frac{d}{p}=\frac{d}{2} .
(a) d=1
(b) d=2
(c) d\geq 3
Figure 1. The admissible region for Strichartz pair (q,p) .
(2)
Decay of potential. To make the potential perturbative in e^{it(-\Delta+V)} , we need at least |x|^{-2} decay of V (see [26] for counterexamples). However, the deformed case b\neq 0 can admit arbitrarily small polynomial decay ( \alpha>0 in the sense of (1.27)). This amounts to different boundedness of the limiting extension (-\Delta-(\lambda+i0))^{-1} and (-\Delta_{b}-(\lambda+i0))^{-1} .
(3)
No spectral assumption. Previous results usually assume the absence of resonance or embedded eigenvalues [59, 20], especially in the scenario of matrix-valued complex-valued potential [4, 25]. They would essentially affect the dispersive behavior, see [20] for more discussion. However, we can avoid that trouble by changing spaces \dot{H}^{\sigma} thanks to those two features of \Delta_{b} . When \sigma varies, the \dot{H}^{\sigma} -decay implies a shift of essential spectrum, while the discrete spectrum basically remains the same by uniform treatment with extended resolvent families. This decoupling effect allows us to avoid embedded exceptional values.
4. Finiteness of discrete spectrum. The operator P_{\rm disc} is the Riesz projection for discrete spectrum of {\mathcal{H}} (see Proposition 4.5). While the discrete spectra may accumulate toward the essential spectrum (see [4, Proposition 2.9]) for Schrödinger operator, the finite rank of P_{\rm disc} is an implication of the self-similar dispersion. This unconditional finiteness of unstable spectrum for linearized operator around blowup can also be proven through semigroup theory (see [49] or [24]).
5. Restriction on \sigma . The condition 0<\sigma-s_{c}\ll 1 is only to ensure the absence of exceptional value on essential spectrum by the decoupling effect.
We also obtain \dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1} Strichartz (Lemma 7.2) as persistence of regularity for linear equation, and generalization to higher regularity is well-expected. However, due to the existence of embedded resonance {\mathcal{H}}(iQ_{b},-i\overline{Q_{b}})^{T}=0 , the Strichartz estimate at sharp regularity is very unclear, even for the radial case44
4
For wave equation, the linearized operator has no embedding eigenmode in radial case, but has one generated by Lorentz symmetry in the non-radial case.
1.5. Outline of the proofs and the paper.
1.5.1. Proof of Strichartz estimates.
We proof Theorem 1.6 in Section 2-5 following the framework of Beceanu [4].
Step 1. Spectral analysis for scalar operators.
In Section 2, we will prove self-similar dispersive estimate and free Strichartz estimate for \Delta_{b} as a self-adjoint operator on L^{2} , extend resolvent families as bounded operators from L^{p^{\prime}} to L^{p} (p>2) and discuss their properties as resolvents. Since {\mathcal{L}}(L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}) is no longer an algebra, we make sense of the resolvent identities and differentiability in a weighted L^{2} space to exploit oscillation in the Fourier space.
Next, in Section 3, we consider scalar operator {\overline{V}}_{2}(-\Delta_{b}-z)^{-1}{\overline{V}}_{1} in \dot{H}^{\sigma} with scalar potentials {\overline{V}}_{1},{\overline{V}}_{2} and prove its compactness and decay in z .
Step 2. Spectral analysis for matrix operators. We come back to the matrix operator {\mathcal{H}} in Section 4. By decomposing V=V_{1}V_{2} , the previous results ensure I+V_{2}({\mathcal{H}}-V-z)^{-1}V_{1} analytic Fredholm as bounded operators in \dot{H}^{\sigma} with z in a region independent of \sigma . Hence by picking \sigma close to s_{c} , we can identify and decouple the discrete spectrum with the essential spectrum, and discuss their spectral Riesz projections P_{\rm disc} and P_{\rm ess} .
We conclude by confirming P_{\rm ess} eliminates all singularities of ({\mathcal{H}}-z)^{-1} .
Step 3. Abstract Wiener’s theorem and end of the proof. In [4], Beceanu proved the abstract Wiener’s theorem which determines the invertibility of an operator in a convolutional Banach algebra through the invertibility of its Fourier transform. Since the Strichartz estimate for e^{i\tau{\mathcal{H}}} can be reduced to invertibility of I-iV_{2}e^{i\tau({\mathcal{H}}-V)}V_{1} in L^{2}_{\tau}\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{y} , it further boils down to the invertibility of its \tau -Fourier transform I+V_{2}({\mathcal{H}}-V-z)^{-1}V_{1} in \dot{H}^{\sigma} and some compactness. Hence the uniform invertibility of ({\mathcal{H}}-z)^{-1}P_{\rm ess} implies a Strichartz estimate for the modified system
\displaystyle i\partial_{t}Z+{\mathcal{H}}P_{\rm ess}+i\mu P_{\rm disc}=F
which captures the evolution of the original system in P_{\rm ess}\dot{H}^{\sigma} and concludes the proof in Section 5.
1.5.2. Proof of \dot{H}^{\sigma} stability
In Section 6, we prove Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.4. We start by introducing J -invariance to connect (NLS) with the spectrally decoupled system. The Strichartz and spectral gap enables us to construct the solution for any given center-stable initial datum through contraction mapping (namely Lyapunov-Perron method, see for example [60]). As a corollary, we have a nonlinear unstable-data map from center-stable initial data to the unstable data leading to blowup (Theorem 6.4). One difficulty comes from the nonlinear estimates in Appendix C, where we take D^{\sigma} derivative for a C^{\beta} nonlinearity with possibly \beta<\sigma by exploiting the non-vanishing of Q_{b} .
For Theorem 1.4, instead of introducing modulation parameters, track its evolution and redo the construction, we use a Brouwer argument to match initial data, inspired by Donninger-Shörkhuber [15]. Specifically, for general initial data, we find the suitable symmetry transformation to shift it onto the blowup manifold constructed in Theorem 1.1. The mode stability Assumption 1.2 ensures the degree of freedom equals the lack of codimensions and non-degeneracy.
This argument not only simplifies the proof by avoiding modulation argument, but also enables more time-independent tools like the Strichartz estimates for stability problem.
1.5.3. Proof of H^{1} stability
Finally, we prove the H^{1} result Theorem 1.5 in Section 7. In order to generalize from \dot{H}^{\sigma} stability theory, we introduce the modified spectral projections for localization and \dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1} Strichartz estimate for improving regularity.
Critical decay of self-similar profile is a universal difficulty for obtaining localized blowup solution (for instance in fluid [18], dispersive [49, 32], or parabolic problems [38]). Instead of introducing time-dependent damped profiles, we apply the approach from [38] to reparametrize the spectral decomposition and localize the eigenmodes from discrete spectrum (Lemma 7.1). Interestingly, this argument also helps with the improvement of regularity, since stable eigenmodes have oscillatory nature and may not belong to \dot{H}^{1} .
The \dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1} Strichartz estimate (Lemma 7.2) follows from differentiating the equation and commutator estimates of residuals. Then like Theorem 6.4, we can construct unstable-data map in \dot{H}^{\sigma}\cap\dot{H}^{1} with modified spectral projections, which can lead to localized blowup datum. Then the proof of Theorem 1.5 comes as in Section 6 for \dot{H}^{\sigma} results.
1.6. Notations.
The notation in this paper is standard. We use \hat{f} or \mathscr{F}f to denote the Fourier transform of a function f , and D^{\alpha}f:=\mathscr{F}^{-1}|\xi|^{\alpha}\mathscr{F}f with \alpha\in\mathbb{R} for the fractional derivatives. We write balls in Banach space X as B_{R}^{X}:=\{x\in X:|x|<R\} . For X=\mathbb{R}^{d} , we omit the superscript and denote B_{R}=B_{R}^{X} , with B_{R}^{c} its complement. The Japanese bracket notation is \langle x\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|x|^{2}} . The energy-critical exponent is 2^{*}:=\frac{2d}{d-2} for d\geq 3 and 2^{*}:=+\infty for d=1,2 .
The weighted \dot{H}^{\sigma} (\sigma\geq 0) spaces with weight \langle x\rangle^{\delta}(\delta\in\mathbb{R}) are written as
\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\delta}}}:=\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta}D^{\sigma-n}\nabla^{n}f\|_{L^{2}} for \sigma\in[n,n+1) , n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .
The Schwartz function space is \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . For the product of Banach spaces X=X_{1}\times X_{2}\times...\times X_{n} , we define its norm as \|f\|_{X}:=\max_{j}\{\|f_{j}\|_{X_{j}}\} . We also denote {\mathcal{L}}(X\to Y) for Banach space of bounded linear operators from X to Y , and {\mathcal{L}}(X):={\mathcal{L}}(X\to X) . The spectrum of a bounded linear operator T is \sigma(T) .
We denote the smooth radial cut-off function to be
\chi(r)=\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}1&r\leq 1\\
0&r\geq\frac{3}{2}\end{array}\right.\in C^{\infty}_{c,rad}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\chi_{R}=\chi(R^{-1}\cdot).
(1.31)
When working with vector-valued functions (say Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2})^{T} ) and corresponding operators (say multiplication by matrix-valued functions V ), for convenience, we keep the scalar notations of Sobolev norms and operator norms.

2. Spectral analysis of \Delta_{b}
This section is devoted to the analysis properties of the deformed Laplacian \Delta_{b}=\Delta+ib\left(\frac{d}{2}+x\cdot\nabla\right) , its semigroup e^{it\Delta_{b}} and resolvents (-\Delta_{b}-z)^{-1} .
To begin with, we notice that
e^{i\frac{b|x|^{2}}{4}}\circ(-\Delta_{b})\circ e^{-i\frac{b|x|^{2}}{4}}=-\Delta-\frac{b^{2}|x|^{2}}{4},
(2.1)
and that -\Delta+V is essentially self-adjoint on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) if V\gtrsim-\langle x\rangle^{2} (see [56, Section X.5, P.199]). Therefore we consider -\Delta_{b} as the self-adjoint operator on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) .
2.1. Free evolution e^{it\Delta_{b}} and self-similar dispersion
Since \Delta_{b} is self-adjoint, we can well-define
the unitary operator group e^{it\Delta_{b}} on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . In this subsection, we will give exact formulas of e^{it\Delta_{b}} and derive the self-similar dispersive estimate and Strichartz estimates for such free evolution.
Lemma 2.1 (Formula for e^{it\Delta_{b}} ).
For u_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , b\neq 0 , we have
\displaystyle(\widehat{e^{it\Delta_{b}}u_{0}})(t,\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle e^{\frac{bdt}{2}}\hat{u}_{0}(e^{bt}\xi)e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\xi|^{2}},
(2.2)
\displaystyle(e^{it\Delta_{b}}u_{0})(t,x)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\frac{id\pi}{4}\mathrm{sgn}(t)}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}|\sinh{bt}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\frac{b}{2(1-e^{-2bt})}|xe^{-bt}-y|^{2}}u_{0}(y)dy.
(2.3)
Proof.
The formula (2.3) was derived in [9, (1.8)] using Mehler’s formula. Here we give another simpler proof using self-similar rescaling of the original free Schrödinger equation
i\partial_{t}u+\Delta u=0,\quad u\big|_{t=0}=u_{0}.
For simplicity, assume b>0 .
Consider the L^{2} rescaling
u(t,x)=\lambda(t)^{-\frac{d}{2}}v(\tau,y)
with self-similar laws similar to (1.3)
\lambda(t)=\sqrt{1-2bt},\quad\tau(t)=-\frac{\ln\left(1-2bt\right)}{2b},\quad y=\frac{x}{\lambda(t)},
so that \lambda(t(\tau))=e^{-b\tau} , \lambda\big|_{t=0}=1 , and \tau\big|_{t=0}=0 . A direct computation implies
i\partial_{\tau}v+\left[\Delta_{y}+ib\left(\frac{d}{2}+y\cdot\nabla_{y}\right)\right]v=0,\quad v\big|_{\tau=0}=u_{0}.
Hence
e^{it\Delta}u_{0}=\lambda(t)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(e^{i\tau(t)\Delta_{b}}u_{0}\right)(\lambda(t)^{-1}\cdot)
(2.4)
as t<\frac{1}{2b} namely \tau<\infty . This yields (2.2) and (2.3).
∎
As in the original Schrödinger case, these exact formulas of e^{it\Delta_{b}} yield dispersive estimates and thereafter Strichartz estimates.
Proposition 2.2 (Dispersive estimates and Strichartz estimates for e^{it\Delta_{b}} ).
Let b\neq 0 , d\geq 1 .
(i)
Dispersive estimates: For p\in[2,\infty] , we have
\|e^{it\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\lesssim_{d,p}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|t|^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)}&|t|\leq|b|^{-1}\\
(|b|^{-1}e^{|bt|})^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)}&|t|>|b|^{-1}\end{array}\right.
(2.5)
(ii)
Strichartz estimates:
Let u(t) be solution of i\partial_{t}u+\Delta_{b}u=F with u_{0} and F regular, we have
\|u(t)\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{t}L^{p_{1}}_{x}}\lesssim_{d}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|F\|_{L^{q_{2}^{\prime}}_{t}L^{p_{2}^{\prime}}_{x}}.
(2.6)
where (q_{i},p_{i}) for i=1,2 satisfies the admissible condition (1.30).
Proof.
The dispersive estimates (2.5) follows interpolation between L^{2}\to L^{2} conservation and L^{1}\to L^{\infty} dispersive estimate, which are direct consequences of (2.2) and (2.3) respectively. Note that (2.5) also indicates
\|e^{it\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\lesssim|t|^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)}.
The result of abstract result of Keel-Tao [31] induces the Strichartz estimates for q,p\geq 2 satisfying \frac{2}{q}+\frac{d}{p}=\frac{d}{2} except (q,p,d)=(2,\infty,2) . For the other indices (q,p)\in\left\{q\geq 2,2<p\leq\infty,\frac{2}{q}+\frac{d}{p}>\frac{d}{2}\right\} , (2.5) indicates that \|e^{it\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\frac{q}{2}}(\mathbb{R}) . Hence Young’s inequality and Hölder’s inequality imply
\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(e^{i(t-s)\Delta_{b}}F(s),G(t)\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}dsdt\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|e^{i(t-s)\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\|F(s)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}\|G(t)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}dsdt
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}\|G\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}\left\|\|e^{i\tau\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\right\|_{L^{\frac{q}{2}}_{\tau}}\lesssim\|F\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}\|G\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}}.
With this estimate, the standard TT^{*} -argument [23] will conclude the proof.
∎
We emphasize that the dispersion decay rate in (2.5) is exponential due to the self-similar scaling.
2.2. Extended resolvent families
Using Laplace transform, we can define the resolvent of \Delta_{b} through the free evolution e^{it\Delta_{b}}
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(z)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}dt,\quad\quad\quad\Im z>0;
(2.7)
\displaystyle R_{b}^{-}(z)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\Delta_{b}}e^{-itz}dt,\quad\Im z<0.
(2.8)
They satisfy the following resolvent properties:
Lemma 2.3 (Resolvent properties).
Let b\neq 0 . For \Im z>0 , the resolvent R^{+}_{b}(z) satisfies (similarly for R^{-}_{b}(z) with \Im z<0 ):
(i)
Boundedness: R_{b}^{+}(z) is bounded in L^{2} and bijective from L^{2} to D(\Delta_{b}) .
(ii)
Inversion:
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(z)(-\Delta_{b}-z)u
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle u\quad u\in D(\Delta_{b}),
\displaystyle(-\Delta_{b}-z)R_{b}^{+}(z)u
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle u\quad u\in L^{2}.
(iii)
Resolvent identities: for \Im z>0 , \Im z^{\prime}>0 ,
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(z)-R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})=(z-z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime}).
In particular, R_{b}^{+}(z) is analytic on \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Im z>0\} as \mathcal{L}(L^{2}) -valued function with derivative \partial_{z}R_{b}^{+}(z)=R_{b}^{+}(z)^{2}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})) .
Moreover, there holds
(iii’)
Resolvent identities for different families: For \Im z>0 and \Im w<0 ,
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(z)-R_{b}^{-}(w)=(z-w)R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{-}(w)=(z-w)R_{b}^{-}(w)R_{b}^{+}(z).
Proof.
This is classical operator semigroup theory [19, Chap.2 Thm. 1.10].
∎
However, the self-similar dispersive estimate (2.5) indicates the integral in (2.7) to converge in {\mathcal{L}}(L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}) for some 2<p<2^{*} as \Im z>-|b|\min\{\frac{d}{2},1\} . Thus we call them extended resolvent family in a larger range of z . The rest of this subsection is devoted to showing these extended resolvent families retain resolvent properties as Lemma 2.3 in a modified sense.
2.2.1. Boundedness
Firstly, we develop boundedness and a Fourier representation.
Lemma 2.4.
For |b|>0 and the operator R_{b}^{+}(z) with \Im z>-|b|\min\left\{\frac{d}{2},1\right\} (or R_{b}^{-}(w) with \Im w<|b|\min\left\{\frac{d}{2},1\right\} ) defined by (2.7) (or (2.8)), we have the following properties:
(1)
Boundedness55
5
We also conjecture the boundedness at the endpoint case p=2^{*} for d\geq 3 .: for p\in\left[2,2^{*}\right) , R^{+}_{b}(z) is bounded from L^{p^{\prime}} to L^{p} if \Im z>-|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right) , and it is continuous with respect to z in the operator norm. Moreover, we have
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{z:\Im z\geq-|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)+\delta}\|R^{+}_{b}(z)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}<\infty,
\displaystyle\quad\forall\,\delta>0;
(2.9)
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{z:\Im z=\lambda}\|R^{+}_{b}(z)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\to 0,
\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,\lambda\to+\infty.
(2.10)
Similar boundedness holds for R_{b}^{-}(w) .
(2)
Fourier representation: Let b>0 . For f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with \hat{f}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying
r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}|\hat{f}|(\rho\omega)d\rho\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),
(2.11)
for some q\in[1,\infty) , we have R_{b}^{+}(z)f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with its Fourier transform given by
(\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f})(r\omega)=\frac{i}{b}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}+\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln\rho}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)d\rho.
(2.12)
Similarly, if f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with \hat{f}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying
r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im w}{b}}\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im w}{b}}|\hat{f}|(\rho\omega)d\rho\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),
(2.13)
for some q\in[1,\infty) , then R_{b}^{-}(w)f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with Fourier representation
(\widehat{R_{b}^{-}(w)f})(r\omega)=-\frac{i}{b}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im w}{b}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{i\Re w}{b}\ln r}\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im w}{b}}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}+\frac{i\Re w}{b}\ln\rho}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)d\rho.
(2.14)
In particular, all these representations hold for f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . Similar formulas holds for b<0 case.
Proof.
We only prove for R_{b}^{+}(z) with b>0 and the proofs for the other three cases are almost the same.
(1) The boundedness comes from integrating (2.5)
\|R_{b}^{+}(z)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\lesssim_{d,p}\int_{0}^{|b|^{-1}}t^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)}e^{-t\Im z}dt+\int_{|b|^{-1}}^{\infty}|b|^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)}e^{-t\left[\Im z+|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right)\right]}dt
We need p<2^{*} for integrability near t=0 , and \Im z>-|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right) for integrability at infinity. The continuity easily follows
\|R_{b}^{+}(z)-R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\|e^{it\Delta_{b}}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}|e^{itz}-e^{itz^{\prime}}|dt\to 0\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,|z-z^{\prime}|\to 0.
The uniformity (2.9) and the vanishing (2.10) both follow the monotonicity and vanishing of the above integral concerning \Im z .
(2) Formally, the representation formula follows (2.2) and (2.7) directly.
\displaystyle(\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f})(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{itz}\widehat{e^{it\Delta_{b}}f}(r\omega)dt
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{itz}e^{\frac{bdt}{2}}\hat{f}(e^{bt}r\omega)e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}r^{2}}dt
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{iz\frac{1}{b}\ln\frac{\rho}{r}}e^{\frac{db}{2}\frac{1}{b}\ln\frac{\rho}{r}}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)e^{-i\frac{\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{2}-1}{2b}r^{2}}\frac{d\rho}{b\rho}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{i}{b}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}+\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln\rho}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)d\rho
where in the third equivalence, we change t to the variable \rho=e^{bt}r\in[r,\infty) with b>0 . Since e^{it\Delta_{b}} maps \mathscr{S} to itself and hence maps \mathscr{S}^{\prime} to itself, the integration on a finite interval is well-defined in \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . Thus it suffices to verify the integral in the first equivalence to converge in the sense of \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) to make this computation rigorous. Using (2.2),
\displaystyle\left|i\int_{R}^{\infty}e^{itz}\widehat{e^{it\Delta_{b}}f}(r\omega)dt\right|
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\int_{R}^{\infty}e^{-t\Im z}e^{\frac{bdt}{2}}\left|\hat{f}(e^{bt}r\omega)\right|dt
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle b^{-1}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{re^{Rb}}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)\right|d\rho
So we can use (2.11) as a dominant function in L^{q} to show dominant convergence. The tail vanishes in L^{q} and hence the computation makes sense.
∎
As an application, we prove the full characterization of \sigma(\Delta_{b}) with b\neq 0 , which is drastically different from the b=0 case.
Lemma 2.5 (Spectrum of \Delta_{b} ).
For b\neq 0 , \sigma(-\Delta_{b})=\mathbb{R} .
Proof.
With the self-adjointness of -\Delta_{b} , we will apply the explicit formula of extended resolvent families to prove \lambda\in\sigma(-\Delta_{b}) for all \lambda\in\mathbb{R} . We assume b>0 and the case b<0 comes in the same way.
If not, then \lambda\in\rho(-\Delta_{b}) and -\Delta_{b}-z is invertible near z with an analytic inverse. We denote R_{b}(\lambda)=(-\Delta_{b}-\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}) , then this analyticity implies
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+i\epsilon)\to R_{b}(\lambda)\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\mathcal{L}(L^{2})
\displaystyle\,\,\mathrm{as}\,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+};
\displaystyle R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\to R_{b}(\lambda)\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\mathcal{L}(L^{2})
\displaystyle\,\,\mathrm{as}\,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+}.
Also recall the continuity and boundedness in Lemma 2.4. Suppose \epsilon<\frac{|b|}{4} , we also have
\displaystyle R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+i\epsilon)\to R_{b}^{+}(\lambda)\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\mathcal{L}(L^{\frac{4d}{2d+1}}\to L^{\frac{4d}{2d-1}})
\displaystyle\,\,\mathrm{as}\,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+};
\displaystyle R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\to R_{b}^{-}(\lambda)\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\mathcal{L}(L^{\frac{4d}{2d+1}}\to L^{\frac{4d}{2d-1}})
\displaystyle\,\,\mathrm{as}\,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+}.
Thus for f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , we should have R^{+}_{b}(\lambda)f=R_{b}(\lambda)f=R_{b}^{-}(\lambda)f , while
\mathscr{F}(R_{b}^{+}(\lambda)f-R_{b}^{-}(\lambda)f)(r\omega)=\frac{i}{b}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}+\frac{i\Re z}{b}\ln\rho}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)d\rho
which may not be zero when the integral is non-zero. This contradiction shows that \lambda\in\sigma(-\Delta_{b}) and finishes the proof.
∎
2.2.2. Inversion property
Next, we come to the inversion property. We first check the strong continuity of the semigroup in the sense of tempered distribution.
Lemma 2.6.
For z\in\mathbb{C} and f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , the orbital map
C_{z,f}:t\mapsto e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}f
is continuous and differentiable from \mathbb{R} to \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with
\frac{d}{dt}C_{z,f}(t):=\mathop{\rm lim}_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\left(e^{i(h+t)(\Delta_{b}+z)}f-e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}f\right)=i(\Delta_{b}+z)e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}f
(2.15)
where the convergence is in the sense of \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and is uniform in finite time.
The same statements also hold if we replace \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) by \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) .
Proof.
The conclusion (continuity, differentiability and uniform convergence of derivatives) in \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) follow direct computations using (2.2). For example, for the continuity at t=0 with z=0 , we compute
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(e^{it\Delta_{b}}f-f)(\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left(e^{\frac{bdt}{2}}-1\right)\hat{f}(e^{bt}\xi)e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\xi|^{2}}+\left(\hat{f}(e^{bt}\xi)-\hat{f}(\xi)\right)e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\xi|^{2}}
\displaystyle+\hat{f}(\xi)\left(e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\xi|^{2}}-1\right).
It is easy to check that each term on the right-hand side is vanishing in the Schwartz space. Then since
\displaystyle\langle e^{it\Delta_{b}}f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}}=\langle f,e^{-it\Delta_{b}}\varphi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}},\quad\langle\Delta_{b}f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}}=\langle f,\Delta_{b}\varphi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}},
the conclusion in \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) follows the one in \mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) .
∎
Now we prove the inversion property within the range of the previous lemma.
Lemma 2.7 (Inverseion properties for extended resolvent families).
Let p\in\left[2,2^{*}\right) and z\in\mathbb{C} satisfying \Im z>-|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right) .
If u\in L^{p^{\prime}} , we have
(-\Delta_{b}-z)R_{b}^{+}(z)u=u.
(2.16)
Moreover, if u\in L^{p^{\prime}} and \Delta_{b}u\in L^{p^{\prime}} , then
R_{b}^{+}(z)(-\Delta_{b}-z)u=u.
(2.17)
Similar identities hold for R^{-}_{b}(z) with z in the corresponding range.
Proof.
First observing that for such p and z in the lemma and f\in L^{p^{\prime}} , we have
e^{ih(\Delta_{b}+z)}R_{b}^{+}(z)f=e^{ih(\Delta_{b}+z)}i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}fdt=i\int_{h}^{\infty}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}fdt
(2.18)
since the integral converges in L^{p} .
Therefore for u\in L^{p^{\prime}} ,
\frac{i}{h}\left(e^{ih(\Delta_{b}+z)}-I\right)R_{b}^{+}(z)u=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{h}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}udt.
As u,R_{b}^{+}(z)u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , the continuity of C_{z,u} and (2.15) for C_{z,R_{b}^{+}(z)u} in \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) from Lemma 2.6 imply that
both sides of the above equation will converge to (2.16) as h\to 0 .
For (2.17), we start from
\frac{1}{h}\left(e^{ih(\Delta_{b}+z)}-I\right)\int_{0}^{T}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}udt=\int_{0}^{T}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}\frac{1}{h}\left(e^{ih(\Delta_{b}+z)}-I\right)udt
for T>0 .
Letting h goes to 0 , the uniform convergence of (2.15) implies that both sides converges in \mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) to
i(\Delta_{b}+z)\int_{0}^{T}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}udt=i\int_{0}^{T}e^{it(\Delta_{b}+z)}(\Delta_{b}+z)udt.
Since \Delta_{b}u\in L^{p^{\prime}} and u\in L^{p^{\prime}} , the right-hand side converge in L^{p} and the left-hand side converges in \mathscr{S}^{\prime} as T\to\infty . Then (2.17) follows (2.16).
∎
2.2.3. Resolvent identity and differentiability
Although resolvent identities formally hold, it is not trivial to see how extended resolvents compose with \mathcal{L}(L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}) no longer an algebra. The following lemma rigorously verifies such resolvent identities. In particular, the composition makes sense as an operator from L^{p^{\prime}} to L^{p} .
Lemma 2.8 (Resolvent identities for extended resolvent families).
Let b\neq 0 . For z,z^{\prime} such that \Im z\neq\Im z^{\prime}>-|b|\min\left\{\frac{d}{2},1\right\} , and for p satisfies \frac{1}{p}<\frac{\min\{\Im z,\Im z^{\prime}\}}{|b|d}+\frac{1}{2} and p\in[2,2^{*}) , we have
(i)
Resolvent identity for the same family:
R_{b}^{+}(z)-R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})=(z-z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})=(z-z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})
(2.19)
holds as bounded operator from L^{p^{\prime}} to L^{p} .
Similar statement holds when we replace + by - and z , z^{\prime} by -z , -z^{\prime} .
(ii)
Resolvent identity for different families: Let w:=-z^{\prime} and suppose
\Im z>\Im w,
then we have
R_{b}^{+}(z)-R_{b}^{-}(w)=(z-w)R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{-}(w)=(z-w)R_{b}^{-}(w)R_{b}^{+}(z)
(2.20)
Proof.
The first requirement on p guarantees R_{b}^{+}(z),R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime}),R_{b}^{-}(w) are bounded from L^{p^{\prime}} to L^{p} according to Lemma 2.4. So the boundedness of composition follows the resolvent identities. We only need to verify the resolvent identity on f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and focus on the case b>0 for example.
(1) First we check the validity of Fourier representation formula (2.12) acting on R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f . Note that \widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f}(r\omega) has mild singularity near the origin:
|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f}(r\omega)|\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z^{\prime}}{b}}&\frac{\Im z^{\prime}}{b}<\frac{d}{2}\\
|\ln r|&\frac{\Im z^{\prime}}{b}=\frac{d}{2}\\
1&\frac{\Im z^{\prime}}{b}>\frac{d}{2}\end{array}\right.\quad r\leq 1,
(2.21)
and it decays faster than any polynomial order at infinity. Thus
r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f}(\rho\omega)\right|d\rho\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r^{\min\left\{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b},-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z^{\prime}}{b},0\right\}}|\ln r|&r\leq 1\\
r^{-k}&r\geq 1\end{array}\right.
for any k\geq 1 , which implies (2.11). Using (2.12) and changing the order of integration, we get
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f\right)(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz^{\prime}}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\int_{r}^{\mu}\rho^{-1}e^{\frac{i(z-z^{\prime})}{b}\ln\rho}d\rho d\mu
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz^{\prime}}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\left[\frac{b}{i}\frac{1}{z-z^{\prime}}\left(e^{i\frac{z-z^{\prime}}{b}\ln\mu}-e^{i\frac{z-z^{\prime}}{b}\ln r}\right)\right]d\mu
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-z^{\prime}}\left(\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}-\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})f}\right).
(2) Now we have |\widehat{R^{-}_{b}(w)f}(r\omega)|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im w}{b}} for f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . Since \Im z>\Im w , the integral satisfies
r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left|\widehat{R_{b}^{-}(w)f}(\rho\omega)\right|d\rho\lesssim r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\langle r\rangle^{-\frac{\Im z-\Im w}{b}}.
So (2.11) holds for \frac{1}{q}\in\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Im z}{bd},\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\Im w}{bd}\right) . Similarly applying the Fourier representation formulas, we obtain
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{-}(w)f\right)(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\bigg[\int_{0}^{r}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iw}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{-1}e^{\frac{i(z-w)}{b}\ln\rho}d\rho d\mu
\displaystyle+\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iw}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\int_{\mu}^{\infty}\rho^{-1}e^{\frac{i(z-w)}{b}\ln\rho}d\rho d\mu\bigg]
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\bigg[\int_{0}^{r}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iw}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)d\mu\frac{-b}{i}\frac{1}{z-w}e^{i\frac{z-w}{b}\ln r}
\displaystyle+\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iw}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\frac{-b}{i}\frac{1}{z-w}e^{i\frac{z-w}{b}\ln\mu}d\mu\bigg]
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-w}\left(\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}-\widehat{R_{b}^{-}(w)f}\right).
In the second equivalence, the integrability of \rho comes from \Im z>\Im w .
And for R_{b}^{-}(w)R_{b}^{+}(z)f , we see from (2.21) and \Im z>\Im w that for r\leq 1 ,
r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im w}{b}}\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im w}{b}}|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\rho\omega)|d\rho\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}&\frac{\Im z}{b}<\frac{d}{2}\\
C_{\varepsilon}r^{-\varepsilon}&\frac{\Im z}{b}=\frac{d}{2}\\
1&\frac{\Im z}{b}>\frac{d}{2}\end{array}\right.
where \varepsilon can be an arbitrary positive number, and from the fast decay at infinity that the left-hand side is controlled by r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im w}{b}} at infinity. These decay and \Im z>\Im w imply integrability (2.13), and the last equality in (2.20) follows similar computation as above.
∎
However, those resolvent identities does not imply differentiability since R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}) is not convergent when z^{\prime}\to z . We address this issue by invoking a weaker topology \mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\delta}}\to L^{p}) to exploit the oscillation in Fourier space.
Lemma 2.9 (Differentiability of extended resolvent families).
For \delta\in(0,1) , \Im z>-|b|\min\left\{\frac{d}{2},1\right\} and p\in[2,2^{*}) satisfying
\delta>\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}>-\frac{\Im z}{|b|},
(2.22)
we have
\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)^{2}f}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,d,p,z,\delta}
\displaystyle\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}
(2.23)
\displaystyle\left\|\mathscr{F}\{(R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})-R_{b}^{+}(z))R_{b}^{+}(z)f\}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,d,p,z,\delta}
\displaystyle|z-z^{\prime}|\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}
(2.24)
where the second inequality holds when |z^{\prime}-z|\ll 1 . In particular,
R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)\to R_{b}^{+}(z)^{2}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\delta}}\to L^{p})\,\,\text{as}\,\,z^{\prime}\to z.
(2.25)
Similarly bounds and convergence hold for R_{b}^{-}(z) with the corresponding z .
Proof.
Again, we only consider the case b>0 . Note that L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\delta}} is embedded in L^{p^{\prime}} by Hölder’s inequality and R_{b}^{\pm}(z)\in\mathcal{L}(L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}) with z in the corresponding range. It suffices to prove (2.23) and (2.24).
1. Proof of (2.23). Following the computation in the previous lemma, we have for f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) ,
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z)f\right)(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz}{b}\ln\mu}\hat{f}(\mu\omega)\int_{r}^{\mu}\rho^{-1}d\rho d\mu
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz}{b}\ln\mu}\ln\left(\frac{\mu}{r}\right)\hat{f}(\mu\omega)d\mu.
Let \psi\in C^{\infty}_{c,rad}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , non-negative and \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)dx=1 . Define its rescaling \psi_{\lambda}(r):=\lambda^{-d}\psi(\lambda^{-1}r) . Also recall the smooth cutoff \chi from (1.31). Now decompose \hat{f}(\xi) by
\hat{f}=g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}
(2.26)
with
\displaystyle g_{1}(\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi),
(2.27)
\displaystyle g_{2}(\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle(1-\chi(\xi))(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\xi),
(2.28)
\displaystyle g_{3}(\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle(1-\chi(\xi))\left[\hat{f}(\xi)-(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\xi)\right].
(2.29)
Then
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z)R_{b}^{+}(z)f\right)(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{i\frac{r^{2}}{2b}-i\frac{z}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)\left(g_{1}(\rho\omega)+g_{2}(\rho\omega)+g_{3}(\rho\omega)\right)d\rho
\displaystyle=:
\displaystyle G_{1}(r\omega)+G_{2}(r\omega)+G_{3}(r\omega).
We will estimate each term respectively. Before that, we specify a small \varepsilon>0 such that
\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}>-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon
(2.30)
and will bound the logarithm term by \ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\varepsilon} .
(1) Term G_{1} . The support of \chi indicates that {\rm supp}~G_{1}\subset B_{\frac{3}{2}} . For r\leq\frac{3}{2} , by estimating \ln t\lesssim_{\varepsilon}t^{\varepsilon} for t=\frac{\rho}{r}\geq 1 and applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\displaystyle|G_{1}(r\omega)|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}
\displaystyle b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\varepsilon}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}
\displaystyle b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}-\max\left\{-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon,0\right\}}|\ln r|\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
The choice of \varepsilon ensures r^{-\frac{d}{2}-\max\left\{-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon,0\right\}}|\ln r|\in L^{p^{\prime}}(B_{\frac{3}{2}}) . Also, p^{\prime}\leq 2 enables us to bound L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}) by L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}) . Consequently, we obtain
\|G_{1}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{b,d,\delta,p,z}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}} .
(2) Term G_{2} . We integrate by parts over the quadratic phase to gain decay of \rho
\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)g_{2}(\rho\omega)d\rho
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left[\frac{-b}{i\rho}\partial_{\rho}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}}\right]e^{\frac{iz}{b}\ln\rho}\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)g_{2}(\rho\omega)d\rho
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-ib\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2}e^{\frac{iz}{b}\ln\rho}\Bigg\{\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)\partial_{\rho}g_{2}(\rho\omega)+\rho^{-1}\left[\left(i\frac{z}{b}+\frac{d}{2}-2\right)\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)+1\right]g_{2}(\rho\omega)\Bigg\}d\rho
where in the second equivalence we exploit \ln(\rho/r)\big|_{\rho=r}=0 to eliminate the boundary term.
Thus with the \epsilon satisfying (2.30),
we have
\displaystyle|G_{2}(r\omega)|\lesssim_{z,b,d,\varepsilon}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(\rho^{-1}|g_{2}(\rho\omega)|+|\partial_{\rho}g_{2}(\rho\omega)|\right)d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}-\varepsilon}\int_{\max\{r,1\}}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon}\left(|(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|+|\partial_{\rho}(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|\right)d\rho
where we bound \partial_{r}\chi by a constant.
Now we estimate the integral term with Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as
\displaystyle\int_{\max\{r,1\}}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon}\left(|(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|+|\partial_{\rho}(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|\right)d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,\delta,\varepsilon}
\displaystyle\langle r\rangle^{-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon}\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}|(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle\langle r\rangle^{-\frac{\Im z}{b}-\delta+\varepsilon}\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}|\partial_{\rho}(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-3+2\delta}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
Noticing that the radial and angular parts of G_{2} are decoupled, we have
\displaystyle\|G_{2}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,\delta,d,\varepsilon}
\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}-\varepsilon}\langle r\rangle^{-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(r^{d-1},[0,\infty])}\left\|\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}|(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}-\varepsilon}\langle r\rangle^{-\delta-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(r^{d-1},[0,\infty])}\left\|\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}|\partial_{\rho}(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-3+2\delta}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\left(\|r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}-\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(B_{1})}+\|r^{-\frac{d}{2}-\delta}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(B_{1}^{c})}\right)
\displaystyle\cdot\left(\|\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{c})}+\|\partial_{\rho}(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})|\cdot|^{-1+\delta}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{c})}\right).
From the parameter condition (2.22), choice of \varepsilon and the estimates (A.1),(A.2) in Lemma A.1, we get \|G_{2}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{z,b,d,\delta}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}} .
(3) Term G_{3} . We estimate the integral part directly
\displaystyle\int_{\max\{r,1\}}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\varepsilon}|f(\rho\omega)-(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,\delta,\varepsilon}
\displaystyle r^{-\delta-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)-(\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}})(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1+2\delta}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
Similarly, using parameter conditions and (A.3) from Lemma A.1, we see \|G_{3}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{z,b,d,\delta}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}} .
These three parts conclude the proof of (2.23).
2. Proof of (2.24). Define
\Theta(t,w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{e^{tw}-1}{w}&w\in\mathbb{C}-\{0\},\\
t&w=0,\end{array}\right.
with t\geq 0 and let
a:=\frac{i(z^{\prime}-z)}{b},\quad|a|\ll 1.
Recalling the computation in Lemma 2.8, we can uniformly write
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)f\right)(r\omega)=-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\mu^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz}{b}\ln\mu}\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),a\right)\hat{f}(\mu\omega)d\mu.
\Theta satisfies simple estimates
\displaystyle\left|\Theta(t,w)-\Theta(t,0)\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|w|t^{2}&t\leq 2|w|^{-1},\\
te^{|w|t}&t\geq\frac{1}{2}|w|^{-1},\end{array}\right.
\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}(\Theta(t,w)-\Theta(t,0))\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|w|t&t\leq 2|w|^{-1},\\
e^{|w|t}&t\geq\frac{1}{2}|w|^{-1}.\end{array}\right.
Like the first part, we decompose \hat{f} into three parts as (2.26)-(2.29) and estimate each contribution G_{1} , G_{2} and G_{3} as
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z^{\prime})R_{b}^{+}(z)f\right)(r\omega)-\mathscr{F}\left(R_{b}^{+}(z)^{2}f\right)(r\omega)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{b^{2}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{\frac{ir^{2}}{2b}-\frac{iz}{b}\ln r}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{i\rho^{2}}{2b}+\frac{iz}{b}\ln\rho}\left[\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),w\right)-\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),0\right)\right]\sum_{k=1}^{3}g_{k}(\rho\omega)d\rho
\displaystyle=:
\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{3}G_{k}(r\omega).
We control each part similarly to the counterpart for (2.23) but with an |a| factor. We only sketch the proof and focus on the additional difficulty from \Theta . Note that \ln(\rho/r)\leq 2|a|^{-1} and \ln(\rho/r)\geq\frac{1}{2}|a|^{-1} are equivalent to \rho\leq re^{2|a|^{-1}} and \rho\geq re^{\frac{1}{2}|a|^{-1}} respectively.
(1) Term G_{1} . Again, G_{1} is supported in B_{\frac{3}{2}} . So we assume r\leq\frac{3}{2} here. Take \varepsilon_{1}>0 such that \frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}>-\frac{\Im z}{b}+\varepsilon_{1} as before and 0<\varepsilon\ll\varepsilon_{1} specified later. We assume |a|\leq\varepsilon .
If re^{|a|^{-1}}>1 , we know \rho\leq re^{2|a|^{-1}} for all \rho\in\left[r,\frac{3}{2}\right] , so
\displaystyle|G_{1}(r\omega)|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}
\displaystyle b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}|a|\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{2\varepsilon}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,z,\varepsilon}
\displaystyle|a|b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}-\max\left\{-\frac{\Im z}{b}+2\varepsilon,0\right\}}|\ln r|\left(\int_{r}^{\frac{3}{2}}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
If re^{|a|^{-1}}\leq 1 , using that e^{-|a|^{-1}}\lesssim|a| when |a|\ll 1 ,
\displaystyle|G_{1}(r)|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}
\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{r}^{e^{|a|^{-1}}r}\rho^{-1-2\frac{\Im z}{b}}|a|^{2}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{4\epsilon}d\rho\right)+\left(\int_{e^{|a|^{-1}}r}^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-1-2\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{2\epsilon+2|a|}d\rho\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle\cdot b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\left[(|a|^{2}+e^{-2|a|^{-1}})\int_{r}^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-1-2\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{4\epsilon+2|a|}d\rho\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle\cdot b^{-2}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon,b,z}
\displaystyle|a|r^{-\frac{d}{2}-\max\left\{-\frac{\Im z}{b}+2\varepsilon+|a|,0\right\}}|\ln r|\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{2}\rho^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
So letting 3\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1} , we can finish the estimate as before with an |a| factor.
(2) Terms G_{2} and G_{3} . For G_{2} , we integrate by parts over the quadratic phase to gain one order decay of \rho with the cost of creating \partial_{\rho}g_{2}(\rho\omega) and having weights \left[\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),a\right)-\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),0\right)\right] and \partial_{t}\left[\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),a\right)-\Theta\left(\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right),0\right)\right] . These new weights (compared with the estimate for G_{2} of (2.23)) can be bounded by C_{\varepsilon}|a|(\rho/r)^{5\varepsilon+|a|} as we do in G_{1} , so it will not affect the estimate if we take \varepsilon,|a| small enough. Therefore, the estimate of G_{2} follows in the same way with a factor |a| and so is G_{3} .
∎
2.3. Improvement of decay and regularity
Recall that (-\Delta-z)^{-1} with \Im z\neq 0 as a convolution with exponential decaying function can retain polynomial spatial decay. A similar effect holds for our resolvent of \Delta_{b} , which will be used to improve the decay of generalized eigenfunctions.
Lemma 2.10.
For d\geq 1 , \delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1) and z\in\mathbb{C} satisfying
\frac{\Im z}{|b|}>\delta>3\delta^{\prime}
(2.33)
then, we have
\left\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta^{\prime}}R_{b}^{+}(z)f\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{b,d,z,\delta,\delta^{\prime}}\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta}f\|_{L^{2}}
(2.34)
And the same bound holds for R_{b}^{-}(z) with the corresponding z .
Proof.
We will prove the equivalent formulation on the Fourier side
\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}\right\|_{H^{\delta^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{b,d,z,\delta,\delta^{\prime}}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}.
(2.35)
Also since \Im z>0 , the L^{2} bound from Lemma 2.4 reduces the task to bound the \dot{H}^{\delta^{\prime}} -norm of left-hand side.
Recall the equivalent expression of \dot{H}^{\delta} -norm (see for example [1, Proposition 1.37])
\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\delta}}^{2}=C_{\delta,d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left|f(x-y)-f(x)\right|^{2}}{|y|^{d+2\delta}}dydx.
(2.36)
We have
\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\delta^{\prime}}}^{2}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,\delta^{\prime}}\int_{|\eta|\geq\varepsilon_{0}}|\eta|^{-(d+2\delta^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\left(|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi-\eta)|^{2}+|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi)|^{2}\right)d\xi d\eta
(2.37)
\displaystyle+\int_{|\eta|\leq\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi-\eta)-\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi)|^{2}}{|\eta|^{d+2\delta^{\prime}}}d\xi d\eta
(2.38)
The \varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d) will be taken small enough. Notice that the L^{2} bound implies |\eqref{eqimprove1}|\lesssim_{\delta^{\prime},d}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}}^{2} . For (2.38), we will show
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi-\eta)-\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi)\right|^{2}d\xi\lesssim_{d,b,z,\delta}|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}|\ln|\eta||\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2},
(2.39)
for fixed \eta\leq\varepsilon_{0} , and it concludes the proof of (2.35) because \delta>3\delta^{\prime} .
We will decompose the domain of \xi to show (2.39). We first let 100\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}}<1 such that 50\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}}<1<(50\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1} .
1. For |\xi|\leq 20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}} . Since \|\hat{f}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}} with \frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\delta}{d} , we have
\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\Im z}{b}-1}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|d\rho\lesssim_{z,b,d,p,\delta}r^{\delta-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\int_{r}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{p}\rho^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
where we used \frac{Imz}{b}>\delta . Then
\displaystyle\int_{B_{20|\eta|^{1/3}}}|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi-\eta)-\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\leq\int_{B_{30|\eta|^{1/3}}}|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,d,p,\delta}
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{30|\eta|^{1/3}}r^{2\left(-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}\right)+2\left(\delta-\frac{\Im z}{b}\right)}r^{d-1}dr\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left(\int_{r}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\rho\omega)|^{p}\rho^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}d\omega\right)
\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,\delta}
\displaystyle\eta^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2}
as required in (2.39).
2. For |\xi|\geq(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1} .
Here we mollify \hat{f} to use integration by parts. Let \hat{g}_{1}(\xi)=\hat{f}*\psi_{|\xi|^{-1}}(\xi) , and \hat{g}_{2}(\xi)=\hat{f}(\xi)-\hat{g}_{1}(\xi) . Then like the estimate in Lemma 2.9, we have
\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)d\rho=\int_{r}^{\infty}\left(\frac{-b}{i\rho}\partial_{\rho}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}}\right)e^{i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)d\rho
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-ibr^{\frac{d}{2}-2}e^{-i\frac{r^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln r}\hat{g}_{1}(r\omega)-ib\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\left(\rho^{-1}\left(\frac{d}{2}-2+i\frac{z}{b}\right)\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)+\partial_{\rho}\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)\right)d\rho
Thus
\displaystyle|\widehat{R^{+}_{b}(z)g_{1}}(r\omega)|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,d}
\displaystyle r^{-2}|\hat{g}_{1}(r\omega)|+r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-2-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\rho^{-1}|\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)|+|\partial_{\rho}\hat{g}_{1}(\rho\omega)|\right)d\rho
So applying (A.1) and (A.2) from Lemma A.1 and using \frac{\Im z}{b}>\delta ,
\displaystyle\int_{B_{10^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}}^{c}}|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)g_{1}}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,d,\delta}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\hat{g}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{c})}^{2}+|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\|\partial_{r}\hat{g}_{1}|\cdot|^{-1+\delta}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{c})}^{2}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,\delta}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2}.
The \hat{g}_{2} part has the same bound as above due to direct estimate via (A.3).
3. For |\xi|\in(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}},(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1}) .
We begin with decomposing \hat{f} into \hat{f}_{-}:=\hat{f}\chi_{(10|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1}} and \hat{f}_{+}:=\hat{f}-\hat{f}_{-} . Then since \hat{f}_{+} supported outside B_{(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1}} , we can estimate the integral
in \widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f_{+}} for r=|\xi|<(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1} like the previous case,
\displaystyle\left|\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-i\frac{\rho^{2}}{2b}+i\frac{z}{b}\ln\rho}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\hat{f}_{+}(\rho\omega)d\rho\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,d,\delta}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2}{3}+\frac{\Im z}{3b}}\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}\left|\hat{f}_{+}*\psi_{\rho^{-1}}(\rho\omega)\right|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{\delta}{3}+\frac{\Im z}{3b}}\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}\left[\left|\rho^{-1}\partial_{\rho}\left(\hat{f}_{+}*\psi_{\rho^{-1}}\right)(\rho\omega)\right|^{2}+\left|\left(\hat{f}_{+}-\hat{f}_{+}*\psi_{\rho^{-1}}\right)(\rho\omega)\right|^{2}\right]\rho^{d-1+2\delta}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
So similarly by Lemma A.1,
\displaystyle\int_{B_{10^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}}-B_{10|\eta|^{1/3}}}|\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f_{+}}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi
\displaystyle\lesssim_{z,b,d,\delta}
\displaystyle\int_{10|\eta|^{1/3}}^{10^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}}r^{-d+\frac{2\Im z}{b}}r^{d-1}dr\cdot|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}+\frac{2\Im z}{3b}}\|\hat{f}_{+}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2}\lesssim_{z,b}|\eta|^{\frac{3\delta}{2}}\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2}
which is the desired bound in (2.39).
Finally, for \hat{f}_{-} , we need to use the interacton. Let \xi=r\omega and \xi-\eta=\tilde{r}\tilde{\omega} written in polar coordinates, and for convenience, define A(r):=r^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{i\frac{r^{2}}{2b}-i\frac{z}{b}\ln r} to write
\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f}(r\omega)=\frac{i}{b}A(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}\hat{f}(\rho\omega)d\rho.
Now we decompose
\displaystyle-ib\left(\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f_{-}}(\xi-\eta)-\widehat{R_{b}^{+}(z)f_{-}}(\xi)\right)
(2.40)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left(A(\tilde{r})-A(r)\right)\int_{\tilde{r}}^{\infty}\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\tilde{\omega})d\rho
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle A(r)\int_{\tilde{r}}^{\infty}\left[\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}-\left(A\left(\rho+r-\tilde{r}\right)\left(\rho+r-\tilde{r}\right)\right)^{-1}\right]\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\tilde{\omega})d\rho
(2.41)
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle A(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}\left[\hat{f}_{-}((\rho+\tilde{r}-r)\tilde{\omega})-\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\omega)\right]d\rho
(2.42)
and discuss their contributions to (2.39) respectively.
(1) Esimate on (2.42).
We plug in a mollified \hat{f}_{-} to get the H^{\delta} -norm directly.
(2.42)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle A(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}\left[\hat{f}_{-}((\rho+\tilde{r}-r)\tilde{\omega})-\hat{f}_{-}*\psi_{|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}(\rho\omega)\right]d\rho
(2.43)
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle A(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\left(A(\rho)\rho\right)^{-1}\left[\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\omega)-\hat{f}_{-}*\psi_{|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}(\rho\omega)\right]d\rho
(2.44)
We only consider (2.43) because (2.44) will follow likewise.
Since \rho\leq(5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1} , |\xi|\geq 20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}} and |\eta|\ll 1 , we have
|(\rho+\tilde{r}-r)\tilde{\omega}-\rho\omega|=\left|(\rho+|\xi-\eta|-|\xi|)\frac{\xi-\eta}{|\xi-\eta|}-\rho\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\right|\leq\left(\frac{2\rho}{|\xi-\eta|}+1\right)|\eta|\leq\frac{1}{5}|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}
where the {\frac{1}{3}} -power is used.
Define
\gamma\left(\rho,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|};|\xi|,\eta\right):=(\rho+|\xi-\eta|-|\xi|)\frac{\xi-\eta}{|\xi-\eta|}.
(2.45)
Similar as the proof of (A.3), we have
\displaystyle|\eqref{eqimp35}|\leq r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{B_{|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}}\psi_{|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}(\sigma)\left|\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)\right)-\hat{f}_{-}\left(\rho\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}-\sigma\right)\right|d\sigma d\rho
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}\left(\int_{B_{\frac{3|\eta|^{1/3}}{2}}}\frac{\left|\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)\right)-\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)-\sigma\right)\right|^{2}}{|\sigma|^{d+2\delta}}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}|\eta|^{\frac{\delta}{3}}d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,b,z,\delta}
\displaystyle r^{-\frac{d}{2}}|\eta|^{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(\int_{5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}^{5^{-1}|\eta|^{-\frac{1}{3}}}\int_{B_{\frac{3|\eta|^{1/3}}{2}}}\frac{\left|\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)\right)-\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)-\sigma\right)\right|^{2}}{|\sigma|^{d+2\delta}}d\sigma\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
where the integration domain of \rho comes from support of \hat{f}_{-} and that |\gamma(\rho,\omega;r,\eta)|=\rho+|\xi-\eta|-|\xi| .
Let \rho\omega=\tilde{\xi} . For any r\in(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}},(20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}})^{-1}) and \eta\ll 1 , it is elementary to check that \gamma(\cdot;r,\eta) is almost an identity map on that domain of \rho . More precisely, it maps B_{5^{-1}|\eta|^{-\frac{1}{3}}}-B_{5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}} into B_{4^{-1}|\eta|^{-\frac{1}{3}}}-B_{4|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}} and \left|\frac{\partial_{i}\gamma(\cdot;r,\eta)}{\partial\tilde{\xi}_{j}}-\delta_{ij}\right|\lesssim_{d}|\eta|^{\frac{2}{3}}\ll 1 uniformly for \tilde{\xi} , r in the above domain and |\eta|\ll 1 , which implies that \gamma is a bijection from B_{5^{-1}|\eta|^{-\frac{1}{3}}}-B_{5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}} onto its range with uniformly bounded Jacobian. Therefore, after further decreasing \varepsilon_{0} if necessary, we have
\displaystyle\int_{B_{20^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}}-B_{20|\eta|^{1/3}}}|\eqref{eqimp35}|^{2}d\xi
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,d,z,\delta}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\int^{20^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}}_{20|\eta|^{1/3}}r^{-1}\int_{B_{\frac{1}{5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}}-B_{5|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left|\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\tilde{\xi};r,\eta)\right)-\hat{f}_{-}\left(\gamma(\tilde{\xi};r,\eta)-\sigma\right)\right|^{2}}{|\sigma|^{d+2\delta}}d\sigma d\tilde{\xi}dr
\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\int^{10^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/2}}_{10|\eta|^{1/2}}r^{-1}\int_{B_{\frac{1}{4|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}}-B_{4|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left|\hat{f}_{-}(\gamma)-\hat{f}_{-}(\gamma-\sigma)\right|^{2}}{|\sigma|^{d+2\delta}}d\sigma d\gamma dr
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,d,z,\delta}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2\delta}{3}}\left|\ln|\eta|\right|\|\hat{f}\|_{H^{\delta}}^{2}.
This concludes the estimate for (2.43). (2.44) follows likewise.
(2) Estimates on (2.40) and (2.41).
Notice that |A^{\prime}(r)|\lesssim_{d,z,b}\left(r^{-1}+r\right)|A(r)| and |\tilde{r}-r|\leq|\eta| . Using the fundamental theorem of calculus to bound the difference term in (2.40) and (2.41), we will gain |\eta| and a factor r+r^{-1} or \rho+\rho^{-1} compared with the original estimate for |\widehat{R^{+}_{b}(z)f_{-}}| . Since {\rm supp}~\hat{f}_{-}\subset B_{5^{-1}|\eta|^{-1/3}} and r=|\xi|\geq 20|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}} , the extra term can be bounded by |\eta|^{-1/3} . Thus we can estimate trivially
\displaystyle\left|\eqref{eqimp31}\right|+\left|\eqref{eqimp32}\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{b,d,z}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2}{3}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{b}}\int_{\tilde{r}}^{(5|\eta|^{1/2})^{-1}}\rho^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\Im z}{b}}|\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\tilde{\omega})|d\rho
\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}
\displaystyle|\eta|^{\frac{2}{3}}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(\int_{10|\eta|^{\frac{1}{3}}}^{5^{-1}|\eta|^{-\frac{1}{3}}}|\hat{f}_{-}(\rho\tilde{\omega})|^{2}\rho^{d-1}d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Here the decay in |\eta| is enough, and in the integration over \xi , we can perform a similar change of variable like (2.45) to make \rho\omega\mapsto\rho\tilde{\omega} with the given r and \eta . This implies the last piece in the bound (2.39) and concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎
2.4. Conjugation with D^{\sigma}
Finally, we mention the simple but important fact about conjugating \Delta_{b} , e^{it\Delta_{b}} and R_{b}^{\pm}(z) with D^{\sigma} . This implies the \dot{H}^{\sigma} -energy decay of e^{it\Delta_{b}} .
Lemma 2.11.
For b\neq 0 and \sigma\geq 0 , we have
\displaystyle D^{\sigma}\Delta_{b}D^{-\sigma}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\Delta_{b}+ib\sigma.
(2.46)
\displaystyle D^{\sigma}e^{it\Delta_{b}}D^{-\sigma}
\displaystyle=
