\displaystyle e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{-b\sigma t}.
(2.47)
\displaystyle D^{\sigma}R_{b}^{\pm}(z)D^{-\sigma}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle R_{b}^{\pm}(z+ib\sigma).
(2.48)
Proof.
The first identity (2.46) easily follows [D^{\sigma},x\cdot\nabla]=\sigma D^{\sigma} .
The other two relations also come from the represntation formulas of e^{it\Delta_{b}} (2.2) and of R_{b}^{\pm}(z) (2.7) respectively:
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}e^{it\Delta_{b}}D^{-\sigma}f
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle|\xi|^{\sigma}e^{\frac{bdt}{2}}\hat{f}(e^{bt}\xi)|e^{bt}\xi|^{-\sigma}e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\xi|^{2}}=e^{-b\sigma t}\mathscr{F}e^{it\Delta_{b}}f,
\displaystyle D^{\sigma}R_{b}^{\pm}(z)D^{-\sigma}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\pm i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\pm itz}D^{\sigma}e^{\pm it\Delta_{b}}D^{-\sigma}dt
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\pm i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\pm itz}e^{\pm it\Delta_{b}}e^{\mp b\sigma t}=R_{b}^{\pm}(z+ib\sigma).
∎
3. Resolvent estimates for \Delta_{b} with potential
In this section, we consider the compactness and decay in z of operator families V_{2}R_{b}^{\pm}(z)V_{1} on \dot{H}^{\sigma} where V_{1} , V_{2} are scalar potentials. From the commutator relation (2.48), it’s equivalent to discuss
D^{\sigma}V_{2}R_{b}^{\pm}(z)V_{1}D^{-\sigma}=D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}R_{b}^{\pm}(z+ib\sigma)D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}
as an operator on L^{2} . The potential will satisfy polynomial decay
|V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\alpha},\quad|\nabla V(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\alpha-1}
(1.27)
for some \alpha>0 as required in Theorem 1.6.
We also remark that for every lemma and proposition involving R_{b}^{\pm}(z) in this section, we will only prove for the case R_{b}^{+}(z) with b>0 , because the other three cases ( b<0 or R^{-}_{b}(z) ) can be done in almost the same way.
3.1. Compactness
In this subsection, we prove compactness results for the extended resolvent coupled with potential. In contrast to b=0 case, here any small polynomial decay of V is enough to guarantee compactness.
Lemma 3.1.
For d\geq 1 , 0<\sigma<\min\{\frac{d}{2},1\} and V\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying (1.27) with \alpha>0 , then
(i)
The operator D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}R^{\pm}_{b}(z) for \pm\Im z>0 is bounded and compact on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) .
(ii)
The operator D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma} for \Im z>-|b|\min\{\alpha,1,\frac{d}{2}-\sigma\} (and D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{-}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma} for \Im z<|b|\min\{\alpha,1,\frac{d}{2}-\sigma\} ) is bounded and compact on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) .
Proof.
(i) From Riesz-Kolmogorov compactness criterion, it suffices to prove
\displaystyle\|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}<
\displaystyle\infty,
(3.1)
\displaystyle\|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R}^{c}}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\to 0,
\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,R\to\infty,
(3.2)
\displaystyle\|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R}^{c}}\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\to 0,
\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,R\to\infty.
(3.3)
(1) The boundedness (3.1) follows from Lemma 2.4, Lemma B.1 and \Im z>0 so that D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}) and R^{+}_{b}(z)\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}) .
(2) For the spatial localization (3.2), we will prove
\|(1-\chi(R^{-1}|x|))D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{2}}\to 0,\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,R\to+\infty
(3.4)
with \frac{1}{2}\geq\frac{1}{p}>\max\{0,\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{d},\frac{\sigma}{d}\}
and \chi(r)\in C^{\infty}_{c,rad}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) to be 1 when 0\leq r\leq 1 and 0 when r\geq\frac{3}{2} . Then (3.2) follows the case p=2 .
From Lemma B.1, we can approximate V by some C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) function and only prove these asymtotics for such good V .
Suppose {\rm supp}~V\subset B_{M} with M\gg 1 and R\gg M . We act the operator on f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . Recall the physical representation of D^{\sigma}
D^{\sigma}h(x)=C_{d,\sigma}\mathop{\rm lim}_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\frac{h(x+z)-h(x)}{|z|^{d+\sigma}}dz.
Note that here VD^{-\sigma}f is supported in B_{M} while |x|\geq R\gg M , so
\displaystyle\left(1-\chi(R^{-1}x)\right)D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}f(x)=\left(1-\chi(R^{-1}x)\right)C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{V(x+z)f(y)}{|x+z-y|^{d-\sigma}|z|^{d+\sigma}}dydz
which immediately implies the pointwise estimate
\displaystyle\left|\left(1-\chi(R^{-1}x)\right)D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}f(x)\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R}^{c}}(x)|x|^{-{d+\sigma}}\|V\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(y)|}{|z^{\prime}-y|^{d-\sigma}}\chi(M^{-1}z^{\prime})dydz^{\prime}
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R}^{c}}(x)|x|^{-{d+\sigma}}\|V\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|D^{-\sigma}\left(\chi(M^{-1}\cdot)\right)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}
Since \frac{1}{q}:=\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{\sigma}{d}<1 , we know \|D^{-\sigma}\left(\chi(M^{-1}\cdot)\right)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim\|\chi(M^{-1}\cdot)\|_{L^{q}}<\infty and hence
\displaystyle\|\left(1-\chi(R^{-1}x)\right)D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}f\|_{L^{2}}
\displaystyle\lesssim
\displaystyle\||x|^{-(d+\sigma)}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}_{R})}\|V\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|\chi(M^{-1}\cdot)\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim R^{-\frac{d}{2}-\sigma}\|f\|_{L^{p}}
which concludes (3.4) and hence (3.2).
(3) Finally, we will prove the Fourier localization (or called equicontinuity) (3.3).
We again begin with some reductions. Firstly, we can replace V by a Schwartz approximation (still denoted by V ) with \hat{V} compactly supported.
Secondly, we apply the semigroup decomposition of R_{b}^{+}(z) (2.7) and remove small intervals near 0 and \infty because
\left\|\int_{0}^{K^{-1}}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}dt\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}+\left\|\int_{K}^{\infty}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}dt\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}=o_{K\to\infty}(1)
for our \Im z>0 .
Therefore (3.3) is reduced to: for any [a_{1},a_{2}]\subset(0,\infty) and V\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , \hat{V}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) ,
\left\|\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}fdt\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}^{c})}\leq o_{R\to+\infty}(1)\|f\|_{L^{2}}.
(3.5)
Via direct computation and integration by parts over the quadratic phase, we get
\displaystyle\left[\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}fdt\right](\xi)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle|\xi|^{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)|\zeta|^{-\sigma}e^{t\left(b\sigma-\frac{bd}{2}+iz\right)}e^{-i\frac{1-e^{-2bt}}{2b}|\zeta|^{2}}\hat{f}(\zeta)dtd\zeta
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle|\xi|^{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\zeta|^{-\sigma}\hat{f}(\zeta)\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{t\left(b\sigma-\frac{bd}{2}+iz\right)}\frac{ie^{2bt}}{|\zeta|^{2}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{-i\frac{1-e^{-2bt}}{2b}|\zeta|^{2}}\right)\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)dtd\zeta
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i|\xi|^{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\zeta|^{-\sigma-2}\hat{f}(\zeta)\Bigg[\left(e^{t\left(2b+b\sigma-\frac{bd}{2}+iz\right)}e^{-i\frac{1-e^{-2bt}}{2b}|\zeta|^{2}}\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)\right)\bigg|_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}
\displaystyle-\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{-i\frac{1-e^{-2bt}}{2b}|\zeta|^{2}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{t\left(2b+b\sigma-\frac{bd}{2}+iz\right)}\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)\right)dt\Bigg].
Suppose {\rm supp}~\hat{V}\subset B_{M} . Then if |\xi|\geq 2M , the support of \hat{V} indicates |\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta|\leq M and hence |\xi|\sim|e^{-bt}\zeta|\sim_{a_{1},a_{2}}|\zeta| . Hence taking R\geq 2M and letting C=2b+b\sigma-\frac{bd}{2} , we have
\displaystyle\left|\left[\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}fdt\right](\xi)\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2}}
\displaystyle|\xi|^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\zeta)|\Bigg[\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}Ce^{Ct}\left(|\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)|+|\xi||\nabla\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-bt}\zeta)|\right)dt
\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1,2}e^{Ca_{i}}|\hat{V}(\xi-e^{-ba_{i}}\zeta)|\Bigg]d\zeta
\displaystyle\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},C}
\displaystyle|\xi|^{-1}\mathop{\rm sup}_{\tau\in[e^{-ba_{2}},e^{-ba_{1}}]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\zeta)|\left(|\hat{V}(\xi-\tau\zeta)|+|\nabla\hat{V}(\xi-\tau\zeta)|\right)d\zeta.
Using Young’s inequality and \tau\sim_{b,a_{1},a_{2}}1 , we get
\displaystyle\left\|\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{itz}fdt\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}^{c})}\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},b,C}R^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|\hat{V}\|_{W^{1,1}}
when R\geq 2M . That confirms (3.3) and hence part (i) of this lemma.
(ii) Let L:=D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma} . We again exploit Riesz-Kolmogorov compactness criterion. Notice that the condition on \Im z enables us to pick
\frac{1}{p}\in\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{d},\frac{1}{2}\right]\cap\left(\frac{\sigma}{d},\frac{1}{2}\right]\cap\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{*}},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\Im z}{|b|d}\right)
(3.6)
Then the boundedness of L comes from
Lemma 2.4 and Lemma B.1
\|L\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq\|D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{p^{\prime}}}\|R_{b}^{+}(z)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\to L^{p}}\|D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{2}}<\infty.
And the spatial localization follows (3.4).
Finally, we need to show the following equicontinuity condition
\left\|Lf(\cdot+h)-Lf\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq o_{|h|\to 0}(1)\|f\|_{L^{2}}.
(3.7)
Similar to the proof of Lemma 3.1, we begin with several reductions. Again, we replace V_{1} , V_{2} by their C^{\infty}_{c} approximations and peel off small neighborhoods of 0 and \infty in the semigroup decomposition of R_{b}^{+}(z) (2.7). In particular, from the compactness of interval and uniform boundedness of e^{itz} , it reduces to consider a uniform pointwise estimate: for any [a_{1},a_{2}]\subset(0,\infty) ,
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|(D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}e^{it\Delta_{b}}D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(\cdot-h)-D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}e^{it\Delta_{b}}D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}f\|_{L^{2}}=o_{|h|\to 0}(1)\|f\|_{L^{2}},
By commutator formula (2.47) and uniform boundedness of e^{b\sigma t} , it further comes down to
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|(D^{\sigma}V_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(\cdot-h)-D^{\sigma}V_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}f\|_{L^{2}}=o_{|h|\to 0}(1)\|f\|_{L^{2}}.
Next, notice that we may further decompose V_{i}=V_{i1}V_{i2} with V_{i1},V_{i2}\in C^{\infty}_{c} for i=1,2 . Using the boundedness \|D^{\sigma}V_{11}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{\frac{4d}{2d-1}}\to L^{2}} and \|D^{\sigma}V_{22}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{\frac{4d}{2d+1}}} from Lemma B.1, it suffices to prove
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|(D^{\sigma}V_{12}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{21}D^{-\sigma}f)(\cdot-h)-D^{\sigma}V_{12}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{21}D^{-\sigma}f\|_{L^{\frac{4d}{2d-1}}}=o_{|h|\to 0}(1)\|f\|_{L^{\frac{4d}{2d+1}}}.
Finally, with the L^{2}\to L^{2} uniform boundedness
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|D^{\sigma}V_{12}D^{-\sigma}e^{it\Delta_{b}}D^{\sigma}V_{21}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}<\infty
and interpolation, (3.7) boils down to
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|(D^{\sigma}V_{12}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{21}D^{-\sigma}f)(\cdot-h)-D^{\sigma}V_{12}e^{it\Delta_{b}}V_{21}D^{-\sigma}f\|_{L^{\infty}}=o_{|h|\to 0}(1)\|f\|_{L^{1}}.
(3.8)
For convenience, we denote V_{12}=:W_{1} and V_{21}=:W_{2} .
Suppose f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , using representation formula of D^{\sigma} , D^{-\sigma} and e^{it\Delta_{b}} (2.3), we can compute
\displaystyle W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f(\cdot)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle CW_{1}(\cdot)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\sinh{bt}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{i\frac{b|(\cdot)e^{-bt}-y|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2bt})}}W_{2}(y)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)|y-z|^{-(d-\sigma)}dzdy
Let
\Phi_{t}(x,y):=e^{i\frac{b|xe^{-bt}-y|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2bt})}},\quad A(t):=|\sinh{bt}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
(3.9)
We can write
\displaystyle(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle C^{\prime}A(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{W_{1}(x+w)\Phi_{t}(x+w,y)-W_{1}(x)\Phi_{t}(x,y)}{|w|^{d+\sigma}}W_{2}(y)\frac{f(z)}{|y-z|^{d-\sigma}}dzdydw
Define
F_{t}(x,y):=W_{1}(x)\Phi_{t}(x,y)W_{2}(y).
(3.10)
Then
\displaystyle(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x-h)-(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle C^{\prime}A(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{F_{t}(x+w-h,y)-F_{t}(x-h,y)-F_{t}(x+w,y)+F_{t}(x,y)}{|w|^{d+\sigma}}
\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{f(z)}{|y-z|^{d-\sigma}}dzdydw
Note that \Phi_{t}(x,y)\in C^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}) and thus F_{t}(x,y)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}) as W_{1},W_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . Suppose {\rm supp}~F_{t}\subset B_{R}\times B_{R} and |h|\leq 1 . Using
|F_{t}(q-h,y)-F_{t}(q,y)|\leq\|F_{t}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}|h|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R+1}}(y)
for |w|\geq 1
and
\displaystyle|F_{t}(x+w-h,y)-F_{t}(x+w-h,y)-F_{t}(x+w,h)+F_{t}(x,y)|
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\|F_{t}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}|h||w|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{R+1}}(y)
for |w|\leq 1 , we obtain the pointwise estimate
\displaystyle|(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x-h)-(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x)|\lesssim_{R,d,\sigma}\|F_{t}\|_{C^{2}}A(t)|h|\|f\|_{L^{1}}
Since \|F_{t}\|_{C^{2}} and A(t) depend on t continuously, this confirms (3.8) and thus (3.7). The proof of this lemma is concluded.
∎
We remark that this lemma used two mechanisms of Fourier localization: for (i) we used the oscillation in time of e^{it\Delta_{b}} , while for (ii) it boils down to a smoothing effect of Schrödinger propagation with limited speed.
3.2. Decay as z\to\infty
In this subsection, we discuss the qualitative decay for R_{b}^{+}(z) as |z| large.
Lemma 3.2 (Qualitative decay of extended resolvent families).
Let d\geq 1 , 0<\sigma<\min\{\frac{d}{2},1\} , and V_{1} , V_{2}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying (1.27) with \alpha>0 . For any \delta>0 , we have for \Im z\geq\delta-|b|\min\{\alpha,1\}
\|D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\to 0,\quad\mathrm{as}\,\,|z|\to+\infty.
(3.11)
The similar result hold for D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{-}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma} with \Im z\leq-\delta+|b|\min\{\alpha,1\} .
Proof.
From Lemma 2.4, Lemma B.1 and the \delta gap, we know D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}R^{+}_{b}(z)D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma} is uniformly bounded in L^{2}\to L^{2} for \Im z\geq\delta-|b|\min\{\alpha,1\} . Then via the reduction as in proving (3.7) in Lemma 3.1 (ii), it suffices to show for W_{1},W_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , any [a_{1},a_{2}]\subset(0,\infty) ,
\left\|\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{b\sigma t}e^{itz}D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt\right\|_{L^{\infty}}=o_{|z|\to+\infty}(1)\|f\|_{L^{1}}.
(3.12)
Following the computation in proving (3.7), we define
\Phi_{t}(x,y):=e^{i\frac{b|xe^{-bt}-y|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2bt})}},\,\,A(t):=\left|\frac{e^{bt}-e^{-bt}}{2b}\right|^{-\frac{d}{2}},\,\,F_{t}(x,y):=W_{1}(x)\Phi_{t}(x,y)W_{2}(y).
(3.13)
and then we can write
\displaystyle(D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}f)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{t}(x,z)f(z)dz
where
K_{t}(x,z):=A(t)e^{b\sigma t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{F_{t}(x+w,y)-F_{t}(x,y)}{|w|^{d+\sigma}}\frac{1}{|y-z|^{d-\sigma}}dydw.
We claim
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\left(\|K_{t}(x,z)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\partial_{t}K_{t}(x,z)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)<\infty.
(3.14)
Indeed, note that F_{t},\partial_{t}F_{t}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}) whose support is independent of t and A(t)\in C^{\infty}([a_{1},a_{2}]) . We can estimate them similarly as in Lemma 3.1 (ii) to conclude (3.14).
Now we are in place to use a contradictory argument to show (3.12). If not, then there exists \epsilon_{0}>0 and sequences of t_{n}\in[a_{1},a_{2}] , z_{n}\in\{\Im z\geq\delta_{0}-|b|\min\{\alpha,1\}\} such that |z_{n}|\to+\infty and for any n ,
\left\|\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{b\sigma t}e^{itz_{n}}D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\geq\epsilon_{0}\|f\|_{L^{1}}.
(3.15)
Firstly, (3.15) and (3.14) indicate a uniform bound of \Im z_{n} since
\displaystyle\left\|\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{b\sigma t}e^{itz_{n}}D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{-t\Im z_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|K_{t}(x,z)||f(z)|dzdt=o_{\Im z_{n}\to+\infty}(1)\|f\|_{L^{1}}.
So we can take a subsequence such that \Im z_{n}\to c_{1}\in[\delta-|b|\min\{\alpha,1\},\infty) and \Re z_{n}\to+\infty . Now decompose
\displaystyle\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{itz_{n}}D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt
(3.16)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}(e^{itz_{n}}-e^{it(\Re z_{n}+ic_{1})})D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt
\displaystyle+\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it(\Re z_{n}+ic_{1})}D^{\sigma}W_{1}e^{it\Delta_{b}}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}fdt.
(3.17)
Evidently, (3.16) is o_{n}(1)\|f\|_{L^{1}} since \mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}|e^{itz_{n}}-e^{it(\Re z_{n}+ic_{1})}|=o_{n}(1) . For (3.17), we first estimate the kernel
\displaystyle\left|\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Re z_{n}}e^{-tc_{1}}K_{t}(x,z)dt\right|
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle(i\Re z_{n})^{-1}\left|\left(e^{it\Re z_{n}}e^{-tc_{1}}K_{t}(x,z)\right)\bigg|^{a_{2}}_{t=a_{1}}-\int_{a_{1}}^{a_{2}}e^{it\Re z_{n}}\partial_{t}(e^{-tc_{1}}K_{t}(x,z))dt\right|
\displaystyle\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},c_{1}}
\displaystyle(\Re z_{n})^{-1}\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\left(\|K_{t}(x,z)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{t}K_{t}(x,z)\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)=o_{n}(1)
where the boundedness comes from (3.14).
These two vanishing integrals contradict with (3.15) and hence close this lemma.
∎
Using the inversion property of R_{b}^{\pm}(z) Lemma 2.7, we have the following stronger qualitative decay for f with better regularity.
Lemma 3.3 (Quantitative decay of extended resolvent families).
For d\geq 1 , p\in\left[2,2^{*}\right) and y>-|b|\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}\right) , we have for f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
\displaystyle\left\|R_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy)f\right\|_{L^{p}}
\displaystyle\lesssim\langle\lambda\rangle^{-1}\left(\|f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right)
(3.18)
\displaystyle\|(R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+iy)-R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-iy))f\|_{L^{p}}
\displaystyle\lesssim\langle\lambda\rangle^{-2}\left(\|f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}^{2}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right)
(3.19)
Proof.
Firstly, from the boundedness Lemma 2.4, we have the left-hand side of both cases bounded by \|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}} with a constant independent of \lambda . We will then focus on the decay of \lambda with \lambda\neq 0 .
Since f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and thus \Delta_{b}f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , the equation (2.17) indicates
R_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy)f=-\lambda^{-1}f+\lambda^{-1}R_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy)\left(-\Delta_{b}\mp iy\right)f.
(3.20)
It indicates
\|R_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda+iy)f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim_{y}|\lambda|^{-1}\left(\|f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right)
which confirms (3.18). This equation and \Delta_{b}^{2}f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) also implies
\displaystyle\left(R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+iy)-R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-iy)\right)f
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\lambda^{-2}\left[R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+iy)\left(\Delta_{b}^{2}+2iy\Delta_{b}-y^{2}\right)f+R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-iy)\left(-\Delta_{b}^{2}+2iy\Delta_{b}+y^{2}\right)f+2iyf\right]
Hence we get the desired decay in (3.19)
\|\left(R_{b}^{+}(\lambda+iy)-R_{b}^{-}(\lambda-iy)\right)f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim_{y}|\lambda|^{-2}\left(\|f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|\Delta_{b}^{2}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right).
∎
4. Spectral analysis of {\mathcal{H}}
In this section, we work on the matrix operator {\mathcal{H}} (1.4) with the following assumption as in Theorem 1.6:
\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}d\geq 1,\quad b>0,\quad 0<s_{c}<\min\left\{\frac{d}{2},1\right\},\\
W_{1},W_{2}{\rm\,\,satisfy\,\,\eqref{eqpotbd0}\,\,with}\,\,\alpha>0.\end{array}\right.
(4.1)
The goal of this section is characterization of the spectrum and spectral projections of {\mathcal{H}} (Proposition 4.5 and Proposition 4.6). As a corollary, we show the singularity in the extended resolvent of {\mathcal{H}} can be removed by applying P_{\rm ess} (Corollary 4.7).
As preliminary, we define the matrix counterparts of \Delta_{b} , e^{it\Delta_{b}} and R_{b}^{\pm}(z) as
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{b}-1&\\
&-\Delta_{-b}+1\end{array}\right)
\displaystyle e^{it{\mathcal{H}}_{b}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{it(\Delta_{b}-1)}&\\
&e^{-it(\Delta_{-b}-1)}\end{array}\right)
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z)
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle\pm i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\mp it{\mathcal{H}}_{b}}e^{\pm itz}dt=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-R_{b}^{\mp}(-1-z)&\\
&R_{-b}^{\pm}(-1+z)\end{array}\right).
Then all the results in Section 2-3 can be easily transferred to the matrix case. In particular, we have the commutator relations from Lemma 2.11
D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{-\sigma}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}+ib\sigma,\quad D^{\sigma}e^{it{\mathcal{H}}_{b}}D^{-\sigma}=e^{it{\mathcal{H}}_{b}}e^{-b\sigma t},\quad D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z)D^{-\sigma}={\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z-ib\sigma).
(4.11)
We also decompose the matrix potential V as
\displaystyle V=V_{1}V_{2},\quad V_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\\
&-1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}W_{1}&W_{2}\\
\overline{W}_{2}&W_{1}\end{array}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad V_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}W_{1}&W_{2}\\
\overline{W}_{2}&W_{1}\end{array}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Then the V_{1} and V_{2} will satisfy (1.27) for order \frac{\alpha}{2}>0 .
4.1. Characterization of exceptional sets
In this subsection, we first use analytic Freholm theory to discuss exceptional values of the operator I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1} and identify a spectral gap.
To begin with, we take
\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{0}:=\frac{1}{16}\min\left\{\frac{d}{2}-s_{c},1-s_{c},\alpha\right\}>0,\\
p_{0}:=\left(\max\left\{\frac{d-\alpha}{2d},\frac{d-2}{2d},\frac{\sigma}{d}\right\}+\frac{\delta_{0}s_{c}}{d^{2}(p+1)}\right)^{-1}\in\left(2,\frac{2d}{d-2}\right),\\
\tilde{p}_{0}:=\frac{2d}{d-6\delta_{0}}\in(2,p_{0}),\end{array}\right.
(4.19)
and analyze I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1} in \dot{H}^{\sigma} for \sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}] for \pm\Im z\geq-\delta_{0}b . We stress that \delta_{0} and therefore the range of z is independent of \sigma , while p_{0} depends on \sigma .
Lemma 4.1.
Assume (4.1). Let \delta_{0} given as in (4.19). For any \sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}] ,
the two families of operators I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1} are uniformly bounded analytic Fredholm operators in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) for \pm\Im z\geq-\delta_{0}b respectively. Moreover, they are invertible when |z| is large enough, and their inversions are uniformly bounded.
Proof.
Using the commutator relation (4.11), the claim can be formulated for
T^{\pm}(z):=I+D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z-ib(\sigma-s_{c}))D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma}
(4.20)
to be uniformly bounded, analytic Fredholm operator families on L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with the same domain of z . For simplicity, we only prove the claim for T^{+}(z) . We denote \delta:=\sigma-s_{c}\in[0,\delta_{0}] and use the p_{0} from (4.19).
(1) Uniform boundedness. From Lemma 2.4 and the choice of p_{0} (4.19),
\mathop{\rm sup}_{\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}]}\mathop{\rm sup}_{\Im z\geq-\delta_{0}b}\|{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{+}(z-ib\delta)\|_{L^{p_{0}^{\prime}}\to L^{p_{0}}}\leq\mathop{\rm sup}_{\Im z\geq-2\delta_{0}b}\|{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{+}(z)\|_{L^{p_{0}^{\prime}}\to L^{p_{0}}}<\infty.
From Lemma B.1, D^{\sigma}V_{i}D^{-\sigma} is bounded from L^{2} to L^{p_{0}^{\prime}} and L^{p_{0}} to L^{2} both for i=1,2 . These two bounds imply the uniform boundedness of \|T^{+}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))} for \Im z\geq-b\delta_{0} .
(2) Analyticity.
Since 4\delta_{0}<\min\{\alpha/2,1\} , (B.3) implies that D^{\sigma}V_{1}D^{-\sigma} is bounded from L^{2} to L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{4\delta_{0}}} . Noticing that 4\delta_{0}>3\delta_{0}=\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{\tilde{p}_{0}}>2\delta_{0}>-\frac{\Im z}{b} , Lemma 2.9 indicates that \partial_{z}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{+}(z-ib\delta) is bounded in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{4\delta_{0}}} to L^{\tilde{p}_{0}} . Combined with the boundedness D^{\sigma}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{\tilde{p}_{0}}\to L^{2}) , we get the analyticity of T^{+} .
(3) Fredholm. With 2\delta_{0}<\min\{\alpha,1,\frac{d}{2}-\sigma\} , this directly follows the compactness Lemma 3.1 (ii).
(4) Invertibility and boundedness for |z|\gg 1 . Since 2\delta_{0}<\min\{\alpha/2,1\} , Lemma 3.2 implies \|T^{+}(z)-I\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})}\to 0 as |z|\to+\infty . Therefore it is invertible when |z| is large enough and the inverse is uniformly bounded.
∎
Now we can define the exceptional sets and values.
Definition 4.2 (Exceptional set).
Assume (4.1) and let \delta_{0} as in (4.19). the exceptional sets of {\mathcal{H}} in \dot{H}^{\sigma} are defined as
{\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C},\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}:I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})\,\,\text{is not invertible}\right\}
(4.21)
and their elements are called exceptional values.
Lemma 4.1 then implies a uniform characterization of these exceptional sets.
Lemma 4.3 (Gap of exceptional set).
Assume (4.1) and let \delta_{0} as in (4.19). Then \bigcup_{\pm}\bigcup_{\sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}]}{\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma} is a finite discrete set. Moreover, there exists 0<\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{0} such that
\left(\bigcup_{\pm}\bigcup_{\sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}]}{\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}\right)\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}:0<\Im z<b\delta_{1}\}=\emptyset.
(4.22)
Proof.
For every \sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}] and each sign of \pm , Lemma 4.1 and analytic Fredholm theory (see for example [57, Theorem VI.14]) indicate that {\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma} is a discrete, bounded (hence finite) set in \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}\} . By interpolation, I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1} is invertible on \dot{H}^{s_{c}} and \dot{H}^{s_{c}+\delta_{0}} implies its invertibility on \dot{H}^{\sigma} for all \sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}] , so
\bigcup_{\pm}\bigcup_{\sigma\in[s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{0}]}{\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}\subset\bigcup_{\pm}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{s_{c}}\cup{\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{s_{c}+\delta_{0}}\right)
is still a finite discrete set, which guarantees the existence of \delta_{1} and (4.22).
∎
From now on, we will only consider \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) so that the exceptional set is away from \sigma\left(({\mathcal{H}}_{b}+ibs_{c})\big|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\right)=\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c}) , which will be identified as \sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}}\big|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}) .
4.2. Characterization of the spectrum and spectral projections of {\mathcal{H}}
In this subsection, we will connect the operator I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1} to extended resolvents of {\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c}+V on \dot{H}^{\sigma} via (formal) resolvent identities (4.24), and then prove the goal of this section Proposition 4.5 and Proposition 4.6. This connection is made clear in the following lemma.
Lemma 4.4 (Extended resolvents for {\mathcal{H}} ).
Assume (4.1) and take \delta_{0},p_{0},\tilde{p}_{0} from (4.19) and \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3.
For \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) , we define
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{\pm}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}\}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm}
(4.23)
and define the extended resolvent families of {\mathcal{H}} as
{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}):={\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1}(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1})^{-1}V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})
(4.24)
for z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} . Then they satisfy the following properties:
(1)
Well-definedness and boundedness: {\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}) . Moreover, we have the following uniform boundedness for every \nu>0
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{\begin{subarray}{c}z:\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}\\
\mathrm{dist}(z,{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm})>\nu\end{subarray}}\|{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})\|_{\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}}<\infty;
(4.25)
\displaystyle\mathop{\rm sup}_{\begin{subarray}{c}z:\pm(\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c}))\geq 0\\
\mathrm{dist}(z,{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm})>\nu\end{subarray}}\|{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})\|_{\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}}<\infty,
\displaystyle\forall\,2<p\leq p_{0}.
(4.26)
(2)
Resolvent: when \pm\left(\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c})\right)>0 , {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})=\left({\mathcal{H}}-z\right)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) is the resolvent of {\mathcal{H}} on \dot{H}^{\sigma} .
(3)
Relation with {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z) :
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})
\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b}(z+ibs_{c})V{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})
(4.27)
\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})V{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b}(z+ibs_{c})
(4.28)
\displaystyle I-V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1}
\displaystyle=(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1})^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})
(4.29)
(4)
Resolvent identity: for z,z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} , we have
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c})
\displaystyle=(z-z^{\prime}){\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c}){\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c})
(4.30)
\displaystyle=(z-z^{\prime}){\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c}){\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})
in \mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}) . Moreover, if z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{-} and z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{+} also satisfy
\Im z^{\prime}-b(\sigma-s_{c})>\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c}),
then we also have
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c})
\displaystyle=(z-z^{\prime}){\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c}){\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c})
(4.31)
\displaystyle=(z-z^{\prime}){\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c}){\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})
in \mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}) .
(5)
Differentiability: z\mapsto{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}) is differentiable on \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}\}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} as bounded operators from \dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{4\delta_{0}}} to \dot{W}^{\sigma,\tilde{p}_{0}} . Moreover, for any \delta\in(0,\delta_{0}) , we define p_{\delta}>2 as
\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p_{\delta}}=\frac{\delta}{2}.
(4.32)
Then z\mapsto{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c}) is also differentiable on \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\Im z\geq b(\sigma-s_{c})\}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} as bounded operators from \dot{H}_{\langle x\rangle^{\delta}}^{\sigma} to \dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{\delta}} .
We remark that the formula (4.24) can be derived formally through resolvent identities. Now we use it as a definition and then check the properties of resolvents via direct algebraic computation.
Proof.
(1) Well-definedness and boundedness. The definition of exceptional set (4.21) and Lemma 4.1 imply that
(I+V_{2}S_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1})^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})
when z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} and it is uniform bounded away from {\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}^{\pm} . Thus (1) easily follows the bounds of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z-ib(\sigma-s_{c})) , D^{\sigma}V_{i}D^{-\sigma} with i=1,2 as in the proof of (1) of Lemma 4.1.
(2) Resolvent. Similarly, when \pm\left(\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c})\right)>0 , Lemma B.1 and Lemma 2.4 imply boundedness on {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}) and {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}) in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) .
The inversion property follows {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})=({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c}-z)^{-1} and direct algebraic computation. For example, we check ({\mathcal{H}}-z){\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(z+ibs_{c})=I (where we denote \tilde{z}:=z+ibs_{c} for simplicity):
\displaystyle({\mathcal{H}}-z){\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\tilde{z})
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\tilde{z}+V\right)\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})V_{1}\left(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})V_{1}\right)^{-1}V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})\right)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle I+V{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})-V_{1}\left(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})V_{1}\right)\left(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})V_{1}\right)^{-1}V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\tilde{z})=I
where every operator except {\mathcal{H}}_{b} makes sense in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) .
(3) Relation with {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z) . Those three identities (4.27)-(4.29) all follow direct computation, with boundedness in (4.29) indicated in (1).
(4) Resolvent identity. The resolvent identities for {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm} follow those of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm} (Lemma 2.8) and direct computation.
(5) Analyticity. Lemma 4.1 and analytic Fredholm theory indicate that (I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})V_{1})^{-1} is a meromorphic \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) -valued function on \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0}\} with finite rank poles {\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma} .
As in the above proof of Lemma 4.1 (2), Lemma 2.9 and (B.3) imply respectively the boundedness of \partial_{z}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}) in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{4\delta_{0}}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}) and boundedness V_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}\to\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{4\delta_{0}}}) , which implies the differentiablity and boundedness for \pm\Im z\geq-b\delta_{0} .
When \pm\Im z\geq b(\sigma-s_{c}) , any triplet (z,p_{\delta},\delta) with 0<\delta<\delta_{0} will satisfy the condition (2.22) of Lemma 2.9. Then the analyticity follows a similar argument.
∎
We are ready to prove the main proposition of this subsection which characterizes the spectrum and corresponding Riesz projection for {\mathcal{H}} .
Proposition 4.5 (Characterization of \sigma({\mathcal{H}}) ).
Assume (4.1). Take p_{0} from (4.19) and \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3. Let \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) and view {\mathcal{H}} as a closed operator on \dot{H}^{\sigma} . The following statements hold:
(1)
Characterization of spectrum:
\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}})=\{\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c})\},\quad\sigma_{disc}({\mathcal{H}})=\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma}.
(4.33)
where \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} is the truncated exceptional set
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}:={\mathcal{E}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\pm(\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c}))>0\},\qquad\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma}:=\cup_{\pm}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\pm}_{\sigma}.
(4.34)
(2)
Regularity of Riesz projections There exists 2<p_{1}\leq p_{0} and \gamma_{0}>0 such that for any \xi\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} ,
the range of66
6
For convenience, we denote
{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})={\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}),\quad{\rm when}\,\,\pm(\Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c}))>0,
namely making it the resolvent of {\mathcal{H}} on \dot{H}^{\sigma} according to Lemma 4.4 (2). So is {\mathcal{S}}_{b}(z+ibs_{c}) .
P_{\xi}:=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z-\xi|=\varepsilon}S_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})dz
(4.35)
and its \dot{H}^{\sigma} adjoint P_{\xi}^{*} are spanned by functions belonging to \dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}^{\prime}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}} .
Thus, each such projection is bounded from \dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}+\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}} to \dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}^{\prime}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}} .
(3)
Spectral projections: Define spectral projections of {\mathcal{H}} onto \sigma_{\rm disc} and \sigma_{\rm ess} as
P_{\rm disc}:=\sum_{\xi\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma}}P_{\xi},\qquad P_{\rm ess}=I-P_{\rm disc}.
(4.36)
Then P_{\rm disc}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}^{\prime}}+\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}}\to\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}^{\prime}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{0}}) and P_{\rm ess}\in\cap_{p\in[p_{1}^{\prime},p_{1}]}\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}) . Both operators are commutable with {\mathcal{H}} .
Proof.
(1) From Lemma 4.4 (2), we know \sigma({\mathcal{H}})\subset\{\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c})\}\cup\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} . Now we show the inverse inclusion and the classification.
Suppose \xi\notin\sigma({\mathcal{H}}) and \Im\xi\neq b(\sigma-s_{c}) . Then by resolvent identities, we can recover (4.24) and (4.29), implying that \xi\notin\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} . This indicates \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma}\subset\sigma({\mathcal{H}}) .
Notice that the operator {\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma is self-adjoint on \dot{H}^{\sigma} with \sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma)=\mathbb{R} from Lemma 2.5 and the commutator relation (2.46). Since Lemma 3.1 (i) indicates compactness of V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z-ib\sigma) on \dot{H}^{\sigma} when \Im z\neq 0 , the formula (4.24) and Weyl’s criterion (see for example [58, Theorem XIII.14]) indicate that \sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma)=\sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}}-ib(\sigma-s_{c}))=\mathbb{R} . That is \sigma_{\rm ess}({\mathcal{H}})=\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c}) . Thus (4.33) is confirmed.
(2) Thanks to the finite-rank nature of P_{\xi} for \xi\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} , its boundedness is equivalent to check the regularity of \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}) and \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}^{*}) .
Since \mathrm{dist}(\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma},\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))>0 , the boundedness of {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c}) from (4.26) indicates P_{\xi}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}) and \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi})\cup\mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}^{*})\subset\dot{W}^{\sigma,p} for any 2\leq p\leq p_{0} .
Noticing the embedding L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma}}\hookrightarrow L^{2-\varepsilon} for some \varepsilon=\varepsilon(\gamma)>0 , it suffices to prove \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi})\cup\mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}^{*})\subset\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma}} for some \gamma>0 .
We first consider \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}) . The range of P_{\xi} for \xi\in\sigma_{dist}({\mathcal{H}}) are composed of generalized eigenfunctions of finite algebraic multiplicity. Take
\beta=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\alpha,1,\frac{d}{2}-\sigma,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma},\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))\right\},
and
\beta_{n}:=4^{-n}\beta.
We claim that if f\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} and ({\mathcal{H}}-\xi)^{n}f=0 , then D^{\sigma}f\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\beta_{n}}} .
Indeed, if ({\mathcal{H}}-\xi)f=0 , then acting on {\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\xi+ibs_{c}) , we get
f=-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\xi+ibs_{c})(Vf).
Due to our choice of \beta , (B.3) indicates that D^{\sigma}(Vf)\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\beta}} and Lemma 2.10 implies that D^{\sigma}f=-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\xi-ib(\sigma-s_{c}))D^{\sigma}(Vf)\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\beta_{1}}} . Inductively, if ({\mathcal{H}}-\xi)f=g with D^{\sigma}g\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\beta_{n-1}}} , then D^{\sigma}f=-D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\xi+ibs_{c})(Vf-g)\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\beta_{n}}} . Since there are only finite exceptional values, each of which has finite multiplicity, we conclude that the existence of \gamma_{0}>0 such that all generalized eigenfunctions belong to \dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}} .
For P_{\xi}^{*} ,
it is the Riesz projection onto {\overline{\xi}}\in\sigma_{disc}({\mathcal{H}}^{*})
with {\mathcal{H}}^{*}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib(2\sigma-s_{c})+D^{-2\sigma}{\overline{V}}^{T}D^{2\sigma} . So for f\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} satisfying ({\mathcal{H}}^{*}-{\overline{\xi}})f=g , we have
D^{\sigma}f={\mathcal{S}}_{b}({\overline{\xi}}+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))\left[D^{\sigma}g-(D^{-\sigma}{\overline{V}}^{T}D^{\sigma})D^{\sigma}f\right].
(B.4), Lemma 2.10 and a similar inductive argument indicate the desired regularity for \mathrm{Ran}\,P_{\rm disc}^{*} and end the proof.
(3) The boundedness follows (2) and the commutability follows the resolvent property from Lemma 4.4(2).
∎
Next, we will derive a weak representation formula for P_{\rm ess} via extended resolvents {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c}) on the essential spectrum.
Proposition 4.6 (Representation of P_{\rm ess} ).
Assume (4.1) and take p_{0} from (4.19) and \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3.
Let \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) and f,g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) such that
f,g\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{\sigma}f,{\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{\sigma}g,{\mathcal{H}}_{b}^{2}D^{\sigma}f\in L^{p^{\prime}}
(4.37)
for some p\in(2,p_{0}] . We have
\langle P_{\rm ess}f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}d\lambda
(4.38)
where the integral converges absolutely.
Proof.
Firstly, we define and estimate the semigroup e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma+V)} on \dot{H}^{\sigma} . It is equivalent to considering the evolution
i\partial_{t}u+{\mathcal{H}}_{b}u-ib\sigma u+Vu=0
in \dot{H}^{\sigma} and bound u(t) with u(0) .
Let v=D^{\sigma}u and act D^{\sigma} on the equation. With the commutator relation (4.11), it becomes
i\partial_{t}v+{\mathcal{H}}_{b}v=-D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}v
(4.39)
on L^{2} .
Applying Duhamel’s formula, a straightforward estimate indicates
\|v(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|v(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(s)\|_{L^{2}}ds.
This estimate easily enables a contraction argument for local well-posedness of (4.39) with the lifespan only depending on \|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})} . Thus the global well-posedness follows, ensuring e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma+V)} a well-defined semigroup on \dot{H}^{\sigma} . By Gronwall’s inequality, the bound implies
\|v(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq e^{t\|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}}\|v_{0}\|_{L^{2}} , namely
\|e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma+V)}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})}\leq e^{t\|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}
(4.40)
where \|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}<\infty by Lemma B.1.
Next, we prove that for y\gg 1 and f,g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying (4.37),
\langle f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\frac{-i}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma+iy)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma-iy)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}d\lambda.
(4.41)
We first choose y large enough such that \|{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(z+ib\sigma\pm iy)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})}\ll 1 by Lemma 3.2, leading to (I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda+ib\sigma\pm iy)V_{1})^{-1} and hence {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(\lambda+ib\sigma\pm iy) uniformly bounded in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) for \lambda\in\mathbb{R} .
The formula (4.41) therefore makes sense. Increase y if necessary to make y>1+\|D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})} . So for any f,g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) satisfying (4.37), we consider the function of t
\langle e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib\sigma+V)}e^{-y|t|}f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\langle e^{it({\mathcal{H}}-ib(\sigma-s_{c})}e^{-y|t|}f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
which belongs to L^{1}(\mathbb{R}) due to its exponential decay at \pm\infty . Using the integral representation of resolvents (like (4)), the Fourier transform of this function is
\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\lambda}\langle e^{it({\mathcal{H}}-ib(\sigma-s_{c})}e^{-y|t|}f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}dt=-i\left\langle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma+iy)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma-iy)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
(4.42)
We claim that this is again in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}) . Indeed, we first expand {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z) with (4.24) to obtain
\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS\,\,of\,\,\eqref{eqpropr31}}=-i\left\langle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{+}(\lambda+iy)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(\lambda-iy)\right)D^{\sigma}f,D^{\sigma}g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle i\sum_{\pm}\pm\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy)D^{\sigma}V_{1}(I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda+ib\sigma\pm iy)V_{1})^{-1}V_{2}D^{-\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy)D^{\sigma}f,D^{\sigma}g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.
Now we apply Lemma 3.3 with p=p_{0} to gain decay in \lambda . Apply (3.19) to the first term and (3.18) to the last two terms after transposing the first {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda\pm iy) onto D^{\sigma}g . Together with the uniform boundedness of (I+V_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda+ib\sigma\pm iy)V_{1})^{-1} in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) and D^{\sigma}V_{i}D^{-\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{p_{0}}+L^{2}\to L^{2})\cap\mathcal{L}(L^{2}\to L^{p_{0}^{\prime}}\cap L^{2}) , for any p\in(2,p_{0}] , we have
\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{RHS\,\,of\,\,\eqref{eqpropr31}}\right|\lesssim\langle\lambda\rangle^{-2}
\displaystyle\left(\|D^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|{\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|{\mathcal{H}}_{b}^{2}D^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right)
(4.43)
\displaystyle\cdot\left(\|D^{\sigma}g\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{p^{\prime}}}+\|{\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{\sigma}g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\right)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
So (4.42) implies (4.41) through the Fourier inversion formula. Note that this estimate also holds for y\geq 0 whenever \{\mathbb{R}+i(b\sigma-bs_{c}\pm y)\} does not intersect with \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\pm}_{\sigma} .
Finally, we will shift the integration contour in (4.41) toward the essential spectrum \{\mathbb{R}+ib(\sigma-s_{c})\} . Lemma 4.4 (5) guaranteed the analyticity of \left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(z+ibs_{c})f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}} on the corresponding half-plane except \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\pm}_{\sigma} , so we take contours consisting of boundaries of [-R,R]\times[0,y] , [-R,R]\times[-y,0] and small circular contours around all exceptional values. Lemma 3.2 and (4.43) with y=0 guarantees the residual to vanish when R goes to +\infty , so we finally arrived at
\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}d\lambda+\sum_{\xi\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}}\langle P_{\xi}f,g\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
which infers (4.38).
∎
At the end of this step, we use this representation formula to show P_{\rm ess} can remove the singularity of the resolvent {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}) .
Corollary 4.7.
Assume 4.1 and take \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3 and p_{1} from Proposition 4.5. For \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) , we have for any p\in(2,p_{1}] , {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess} is a well-defined bounded operator from \dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}} to \dot{W}^{\sigma,p} for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) , and has the uniform bound
\mathop{\rm sup}_{z:\Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})}\left\|{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}\right\|_{\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}}<\infty.
(4.44)
Proof.
Fix a p\in(2,p_{1}] . We will use the representation formula (4.38), boundedness of P_{\rm ess}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}}) and resolvent identities (4.30) (4.31). Recalling the boundedness (4.26), we only need to estimate for z close to \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{-}_{\sigma} . In particular, we can also assume \Im z-b(\sigma-s_{c})\leq-\delta_{2} for some \delta_{2}>0 .
Firstly, we check the validity of the representation formula
\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
(4.45)
for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})-\delta_{2} , z\notin\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{-}_{\sigma} and f,g\in\mathscr{S} . Since77
7
Here the adjoint operator are in the sense of L^{2} rather than \dot{H}^{\sigma} .
\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}=\left\langle D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})D^{-\sigma}D^{\sigma}P_{\rm ess}f,D^{\sigma}g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left\langle D^{\sigma}P_{\rm ess}f,(D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})D^{-\sigma})^{*}D^{\sigma}g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left\langle P_{\rm ess}f,D^{-\sigma}(D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})D^{-\sigma})^{*}D^{\sigma}g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
It suffices to prove f and G:=D^{-\sigma}(D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})D^{-\sigma})^{*}D^{\sigma}g satisfying (4.37).
From the resolvent property Lemma 4.4 (2), we have
\displaystyle(D^{\sigma}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})D^{-\sigma})^{*}=\left[({\mathcal{H}}_{b}+ib(\sigma-s_{c})-z+D^{\sigma}VD^{-\sigma})^{-1}\right]^{*}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ib(\sigma-s_{c})-{\overline{z}}+D^{-\sigma}{\overline{V}}^{T}D^{\sigma})^{-1}=:({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2})
where A:=D^{-\sigma}{\overline{V}}^{T}D^{\sigma} and \zeta:=ib(\sigma-s_{c})+{\overline{z}} which satisfies \Im\zeta\leq-\delta_{2} .
From (B.4), we know for 0\leq\gamma\ll 1 , A\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}\to L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma}}) . Now from resolvent identities
({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)=-({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}A{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)=-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)A({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1},
we have
({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}={\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)A{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)+{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta)A({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}A{\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\zeta).
Using Lemma 2.10, the boundedness of A above and
g\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , we can find 0<\tilde{\gamma}\ll 1 , 0<\tilde{p}-2\ll 1 such that
D^{\sigma}G=({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}D^{\sigma}g\in L^{2}_{\langle x\rangle^{\tilde{\gamma}}}\cap L^{\tilde{p}} .
Also noticing that {\mathcal{H}}_{b}({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1}=I+(\zeta-A)({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-\zeta+A)^{-1} , we know G satisfies G\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,\tilde{p}}\cap\dot{W}^{\sigma,\tilde{p}^{\prime}} and {\mathcal{H}}_{b}D^{\sigma}G\in L^{\tilde{p}^{\prime}} . With f\in\mathscr{S} , this confirms f and G satisfying (4.37) and therefore the validity of (4.45).
Now for z_{0} with \Im z_{0}\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})-\delta_{2} , z_{0}\notin\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{-}_{\sigma} , we apply (4.45) and the resolvent property of {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{0}+ibs_{c}) to get
\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{0}+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{0}+ibs_{c})\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle\frac{1}{z_{0}-(\lambda+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
where the integral converges absolutely due to Proposition 4.6. Now
fix an arbitrary z_{1}\in\{\Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})-\delta_{2}\}-\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{-}_{\sigma} so that {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{0}+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess} is bounded from \dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}} to \dot{W}^{\sigma,p} . For any other z_{2}\in\{\Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})-\delta_{2}\} , we have
\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{1}+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}-\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{2}+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}f,g\right\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg\langle\frac{z_{2}-z_{1}}{[z_{1}-(\lambda+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))][z_{2}-(\lambda+ib(\sigma-s_{c}))]}
\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{+}(\lambda+ib\sigma)-{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\right)f,g\bigg\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}.
The \langle\lambda\rangle^{-2} decay and uniform boundedness of {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{\pm}(\lambda+ib\sigma)\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p}) indicate the boundedness of {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z_{2}+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess} , particularly when z_{2} is close to \tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{-}_{\sigma} . This enables us to extend the definition {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess} to z\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma}} and implies the uniform boundedness.
∎
5. Abstract Wiener’s Theorem and Proof of Strichartz estimates
In this section, we will prove Theorem 1.6. We first recall the abstract Wiener’s theorem of Beceanu from [4] (also [3]), and then present the proof of our Strichartz estimates by verifying conditions for a modified Schrödinger system.
5.1. Abstract Wiener’s Theorem
In this subsection, we introduce the setting and abstract tool in Beceanu’s work [4, 3], and then present a version for our later use.
Let H be a complex Hilbert space, \mathcal{L}(H) be the space of bounded linear operators from H to itself, and L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H) be the Banach space of operator-valued L^{1} functions on \mathbb{R} . We add a convolutional unit
I_{K}:=\delta_{t=0}I_{H}
to define the normed vector space K
\displaystyle K
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H)\oplus\mathbb{C}\{I_{K}\};
(5.1)
\displaystyle\|\mu+aI_{K}\|_{K}
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle\|\mu\|_{L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H)}+|a|,\qquad\forall a\in\mathbb{C},\,\,\mu\in L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H).
(5.2)
Then it satisfies the following properties:
Lemma 5.1 (Properties of K ).
Let K defined as (5.1). Then the following statements are true.
(1)
K is an unital Banach algebra.
(2)
Multiplication by bounded continuous functions is bounded on K :
\|fk\|_{K}\leq\|f\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R})}\|k\|_{K}.
(5.3)
(3)
If k=\mu(t)+aI_{K}\in K , then k^{*}=\mu(t)^{*}+{\overline{a}}I_{K}\in K as well.
(4)
The Fourier transform is well-defined by
\hat{k}(\lambda):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\lambda}k(t)dt,
(5.4)
which satisfies \|\hat{k}\|_{C^{0}_{\lambda}\mathcal{L}(H)}\leq\|k\|_{K} .
Proof.
The algebra structure is given by L^{1}_{t} -convolution
(\mu+aI_{K})*(\nu+bI_{K})=\mu*\nu+a\nu+b\mu+abI_{K}.
Then all these properties are trivial.
∎
We stress that the invertibility of k\in K means there exists k^{-1}\in K such that k*k^{-1}=k^{-1}*k=I_{K} .
Now we are in place to state the abstract Wiener’s theorem, a sufficient condition for k\in K to be invertible characterized by invertibility of \hat{k}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{L}(H) for all \lambda . We also collect the criterion for the upper triangular property (meaning that A\in K is supported on [0,\infty) ) to be preserved under inversion.
Theorem 5.2 ([3, Theorem 3.3], [4, Theorem 2.3], ).
Let K be the operator algebra of (5.1). If A\in K is invertible, then \hat{A}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{L}(H) is invertible for every \lambda\in\mathbb{R} . Conversely, assume \hat{A}(\lambda) is invertible for each \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , A-I_{K}=L\in L^{1}_{t}(\mathcal{L}(H)) , and
\mathop{\rm lim}_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|L(\cdot+\varepsilon)-L\|_{K}=0,\quad\mathop{\rm lim}_{R\to\infty}\|1-\psi(t/R)L(t)\|_{K}=0
(5.5)
where \psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([-2,2]) satisfies \psi(t)=1 for t\in[-1,1] . Then A is invertible and A^{-1}-I_{K}\in L^{1}_{t}(\mathcal{L}(H)) .
Lemma 5.3 ([3, Lemma 3.4], [4, Lemma 2.5]).
Given A\in K upper triangular with A^{-1}\in K , A^{-1} is upper triangular if and only if \hat{A} can be extended to a weakly analytic family of invertible operators in the lower half-plane (namely it is continuous up to the boundary, uniformly bounded, and has uniformly bounded inverse).
The following lemma was also implicitly used in Beceanu’s work [4]. It bridges such invertibility with our Strichartz estimates. We present a proof for completeness.
Lemma 5.4.
If A,A^{-1}\in K and both are upper triangular, then \int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)ds\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H)) has an inversion \int_{0}^{t}A^{-1}(t-s)ds\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H)) .
Proof.
We first show the boundedness. Since A=I_{K}+L with L\in L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H) , for u\in L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H) ,
\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)u(s)ds\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H)}
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\|L(t-s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}\|u(s)\|_{H}ds\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}+\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H)}=\|A\|_{K}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),H)}
from Young’s inequality. And similar for \int_{0}^{t}A^{-1}(t-s)ds . That \int_{0}^{t}A^{-1}(t-s)ds is the inverse of \int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)ds can be checked using A^{-1}*A=A*A^{-1}=I_{K} .
∎
To conclude this subsection, we present the following version of abstract Wiener’s theorem for operators with exponential decay in t .
Corollary 5.5 (Abstract Wiener’s theorem with exponential decay).
Let K be the Banach algebra of (5.1) and \epsilon\in\mathbb{R} . If A=I_{K}+L\in K is upper triangular with L\in L^{1}_{t,loc}\mathcal{L}(H) and satisfies
(1)
\|L\|_{L^{1}_{t}e^{\epsilon t}\mathcal{L}(H)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|L(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}e^{\epsilon t}dt<\infty .
(2)
\mathop{\rm lim}_{\epsilon\to 0}\|L(\cdot+\epsilon)-L\|_{L^{1}_{t}e^{\epsilon t}\mathcal{L}(H)}=0.
(3)
The Fourier transform \hat{A}(\lambda) can be extended to a weakly analytic family of invertible operators on H for \{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Im z\leq\epsilon\} .
Then \int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)ds\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),e^{\epsilon t}H)) has inversion \int_{0}^{t}A^{-1}(t-s)ds\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),e^{\epsilon t}H)) .
Proof.
Let B(t):=A(t)e^{\epsilon t} . Then B=I_{K}+L_{B} is upper triangular with L_{B}\in L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H) . Since
\hat{B}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda}e^{\epsilon t}A(t)dt=\hat{A}(\lambda+i\epsilon)
is a weak analytic family in the lower-half plane,
B meets the conditions of Theorem 5.2 (the second limit in (5.5) follows (1) here), Lemma 5.3 and Lemma 5.4. Hence there exists B^{-1}\in K . For \mu,\nu\in K , a computation as in Lemma 5.4 indicates
\displaystyle\left((e^{-\epsilon(\cdot)}\mu)*(e^{-\epsilon(\cdot)}\nu)\right)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\epsilon(t-\tau)}(\mu*\nu)(t-\tau)d\tau.
Thus B^{-1}*(e^{\epsilon(\cdot)}A)=(e^{\epsilon(\cdot)}A)*B^{-1}=I_{K} implies (e^{-\epsilon(\cdot)}B^{-1})*A=A*(e^{-\epsilon(\cdot)}B^{-1})=I_{K} . By the uniqueness of inversion in K , we know B^{-1}=e^{\epsilon(\cdot)}A^{-1}\in L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H) . Finally, for the boundedness, by acting \int_{0}^{t}e^{\epsilon(t-s)}A(t-s)ds on e^{\epsilon(\cdot)}u , Lemma 5.4 indicates
\left\|\int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)ds\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),e^{\epsilon t}H))}\leq\|e^{\epsilon(\cdot)}A\|_{L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{L}(H)}<\infty.
Replacing A by A^{-1} , we obtain the same bound which concludes the proof.
∎
5.2. Modified Schrödinger system and Proof of Theorem 1.6
We consider the modified system
i\partial_{t}\tilde{Z}+{\mathcal{H}}P_{\rm ess}\tilde{Z}+i\mu P_{\rm disc}\tilde{Z}=\tilde{F}
(5.6)
where \mu>b(\sigma-s_{c}) and P_{\rm disc} and P_{\rm ess} are spectral projections of {\mathcal{H}} defined in (4.36). Our aim of this section is to apply Corollary 4.7 to verify the condition in Corollary 5.5 and prove the Strichartz estimate.
As a preparation, since
{\mathcal{H}}P_{\rm ess}+i\mu P_{\rm disc}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c}+V-P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu)=:{\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c}+\tilde{V},
(5.7)
we first derive a decomposition of the modified potential \tilde{V}:=V-P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu) .
Lemma 5.6 (Decomposition of modified potential).
Assume (4.1) and take \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3 and p_{1} from Proposition 4.5.
For \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) and \mu>b(\sigma-s_{c}) , there exist p_{2}\in(2,2^{*}) , \gamma_{2}>0 and a decomposition
\tilde{V}=\tilde{V}_{1}\tilde{V}_{2}
(5.8)
such that \tilde{V_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma},\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}\cap\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{2}}}) and \tilde{V}_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}}+\dot{H}^{\sigma},\dot{H}^{\sigma}) .
Proof.
We will specify 0<\gamma_{1}<\min\{\alpha,\gamma_{0}\} with \gamma_{0} from Proposition 4.5 and decompose trivially
\tilde{V_{1}}=\langle x\rangle^{-\gamma_{1}},\quad\tilde{V_{2}}=\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}\left(V-P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu)\right).
(5.9)
Lemma B.1 and (B.3) implies \tilde{V}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma},\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}\cap\dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{2}}}) and \langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}V\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}}+\dot{H}^{\sigma},\dot{H}^{\sigma}) for some p_{2}>2 and \gamma_{2}>0 . Notice that for \xi\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma}=\sigma_{disc}({\mathcal{H}}) ,
P_{\xi}^{1}:=P_{\xi}({\mathcal{H}}-\xi)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|z-\xi|=\varepsilon}(z-\xi){\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c})dz
satisfies P_{\xi}^{1}P_{\xi}=P_{\xi}P_{\xi}^{1}=P_{\xi}^{1} by functional calculus (see for example [64, Theorem V.8.1]) and similar for (P_{\xi}^{1})^{*} and P_{\xi}^{*} . In particular, we have \mathrm{Ran}P_{\xi}^{1}\subset\mathrm{Ran}P_{\xi} and \mathrm{Ran}(P_{\xi}^{1})^{*}\subset\mathrm{Ran}P_{\xi}^{*} . Thus Proposition 4.5 indicates that P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu) is bounded from \dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}}+\dot{H}^{\sigma} to \dot{H}^{\sigma}_{\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}} and the bound of \tilde{V_{2}} follows (B.5).
∎
Then we can define the resolvent of (5.7) and derive its relation with {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}(\lambda+ibs_{c}) and {\mathcal{S}}_{b}(\lambda+ibs_{c}) . A formal computation suggests that
(P_{\rm ess}{\mathcal{H}}+i\mu P_{\rm disc}-z)\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}-(z-i\mu)^{-1}P_{\rm disc}\right)=I.
(5.10)
This motivates the following definition and lemma.
Lemma 5.7 (Extended resolvents for \tilde{{\mathcal{H}}} ).
Assume (4.1) and take \delta_{1} from Lemma 4.3.
For \sigma\in(s_{c},s_{c}+\delta_{1}) , \mu>b(\sigma-s_{c}) and \tilde{V}_{1} , \tilde{V}_{2} obtained in Lemma 5.6, we define
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}^{-}_{b,V}(z+ibs_{c}):={\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}-(z-i\mu)^{-1}P_{\rm disc}
(5.11)
for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) . It satisfies the following properties.
(1)
Uniform boundedness: for some p_{3}\in(2,2^{*}) , \tilde{S}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}) is well-defined and uniformly bounded in \mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{3}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{3}}) for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) .
(2)
Relation with {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-} : the following identities hold for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c})
\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})={\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})-
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1}(I+\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1})^{-1}\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})
(5.12)
\displaystyle I-\tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1}
\displaystyle=(I+\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1})^{-1}.
(5.13)
Moreover, I+\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} and I-\tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} are both analytic and uniformly bounded in \mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) for \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) .
Proof.
Firstly we discuss the commutability issue. Let z\in\mathbb{C}-\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} with \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) . The resolvent identities (4.30) and (4.31) indicate that {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}) is commutable with all {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}(z^{\prime}+ibs_{c}) for \Im z^{\prime}\neq b(\sigma-s_{c}) and z^{\prime}\notin\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\sigma} . This implies
P_{\rm disc}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})={\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm disc}
and hence
P_{\rm ess}{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})={\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})P_{\rm ess}.
(5.14)
From the uniform boundedness Corollary 4.7, the identity (5.14) makes sense for all \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) .
From now on, we suppose z\in\mathbb{C} only satisfying \Im z\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) and take p_{3}=\min\{p_{1},p_{2}\} with p_{1} from Proposition 4.5 and p_{2} from Lemma 5.6. Then Lemma 4.4 and Corollary 4.7 imply (1).
For (2), we claim the following resolvent identities
\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})
(5.15)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}),
(5.16)
which imply (5.12) and (5.13) easily.
These two resolvent identities will follow some computation involving the above commutability and inversion property of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-} . Take (5.15) as an example. From definitions of \tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-} , {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-} , \tilde{V} and identities (4.27), (4.28) and (5.14), we have (the variable z+ibs_{c} of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-} , {\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-} or \tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-} are omitted in the following computation for convenience)
\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS\,\,of\,\,\eqref{eqSbVt3}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}\right)P_{\rm ess}-\left((z-i\mu)^{-1}+{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}\right)P_{\rm disc},
\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS\,\,of\,\,\eqref{eqSbVt3}}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}\left(V-P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu)\right)\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}P_{\rm ess}-(z-i\mu)^{-1}P_{\rm disc}\right)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}V{\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}P_{\rm ess}-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}\left[-V(z-i\mu)^{-1}+({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu)(z-i\mu)^{-1}\right]P_{\rm disc}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{S}}_{b,V}^{-}-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}\right)P_{\rm ess}-{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}P_{\rm disc}-(z-i\mu)^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-z-ibs_{c})P_{\rm disc}
Noticing that for u\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{3}^{\prime}} , we have P_{\rm disc}u\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{3}^{\prime}} and {\mathcal{H}}_{b}P_{\rm disc}u\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{3}^{\prime}} since P_{\rm disc}u is a generalized eigenfunction of {\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c}+V . Then the last term is just (z-i\mu)^{-1}P_{\rm disc} by the inversion property (2.17), and (5.15) follows. The identity (5.16) comes similarly with (2.16).
Finally, we prove the analyticity and uniform boundedness of I+\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} and I-\tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} . We first see the analyticity of \tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} by specifying a 0<\gamma_{3}\leq\gamma_{2} small enough such that p_{\gamma_{3}}:=\frac{2d}{d-\gamma_{3}}\leq p_{3} . Then differentiability of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}) (Proposition 4.5 (5)) and boundedness of \tilde{V}_{1} and \tilde{V}_{2} (Lemma 5.7) imply the desired analyticity.
Next, Lemma 3.1 (ii) and finite-rankness of P_{\rm disc} easily indicate that \tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1} is a compact operator on \dot{H}^{\sigma} . Hence we can use a similar argument as in Lemma 4.1 plus the uniform boundedness of \tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{b,V}^{-}(z+ibs_{c}) to finish the proof.
∎
Now we are ready to prove the Strichartz estimate.
Proof of Theorem 1.6.
Step 1. Application of abstract Wiener’s theorem.
Let H=\dot{H}^{\sigma} and K be the Banach algebra as in (5.1).
Define
L(t):=-i\tilde{V}_{2}e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}\tilde{V}_{1}
(5.17)
for t\geq 0 and
A=I_{K}+L.
(5.18)
We will check the three conditions in Corollary 5.5 with \epsilon=b(\sigma-s_{c}) to apply it.
(1) Firstly, the dispersive estimate (2.5) and commutator relation (2.47) indicate that e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}\in L^{1}_{t}e^{b(\sigma-s_{c})t}\mathcal{L}(\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}\to\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}}) . Combined with Lemma 5.6, we have L\in L^{1}_{t}e^{b(\sigma-s_{c})t}\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) and hence A\in K is upper triangular.
(2) Next, we show that for any [a_{1},a_{2}]\in(0,\infty) ,
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\|L(\cdot+\epsilon)-L(\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma})}=o_{\epsilon}(1).
(5.19)
It implies the second condition thanks to the integrability L\in L^{1}_{t}e^{b(\sigma-s_{c})t}\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\sigma}) . Recall (5.9) that
\tilde{V}_{2}=\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}V+\left(-\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu)\right)=:\tilde{V}_{2,1}+\tilde{V}_{2,2}.
We will treat these two parts separately.
(2.a) The multiplication part \tilde{V}_{2,1}:=\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}V . We first approximate \tilde{V}_{1} and \tilde{V}_{2,1} by W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathscr{S} with \hat{W}_{1},\hat{W}_{2} compactly supported. Supposing \hat{W}_{1},\hat{W}_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{M}) with M>0 , we consider \epsilon<\min\{(10b)^{-1},(2e^{b(a_{2}+1)}M)^{-4}\} and let R=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}} . We decompose for any t\in[a_{1},a_{2}] as
\displaystyle\|W_{2}(e^{i(t+\epsilon)({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}-e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})})W_{1}f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
(5.20)
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\left\|W_{2}\chi(R^{-1}D)(e^{i(t+\epsilon)({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}-e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})})W_{1}f\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
(5.21)
\displaystyle+
\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\left\|W_{2}(1-\chi(R^{-1}D))e^{i(t\pm\frac{\epsilon}{2})({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}W_{1}f\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}
(5.22)
where \chi is the smooth cutoff from (1.31).
For the low-frequency part (5.21), notice that
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(\chi(R^{-1}D)(e^{i(t+\epsilon)\Delta_{b}}-e^{it\Delta_{b}})W_{1}f\right)(\eta)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\chi(R^{-1}\eta)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\left(e^{b(t+\epsilon)\frac{d}{2}}e^{-i\frac{e^{2b(t+\epsilon)}-1}{2b}|\eta|^{2}}\hat{W}_{1}(e^{b(t+\epsilon)}\eta-\zeta)\right)
\displaystyle\quad\quad-\left(e^{bt\frac{d}{2}}e^{-i\frac{e^{2bt}-1}{2b}|\eta|^{2}}\hat{W}_{1}(e^{bt}\eta-\zeta)\right)\bigg]|\zeta|^{-\sigma}\widehat{D^{\sigma}f}(\zeta)d\zeta
where the absolute value of the bracket can be bounded by
2(b+b^{-1})\epsilon R^{2}e^{b(a_{2}+1)\left(\frac{d}{2}+2\right)}\left[\int_{0}^{1}|\nabla\hat{W}_{1}(e^{b(t+\epsilon s)}\eta-\zeta)|ds+\sum_{\pm}|\hat{W}_{1}(e^{b(t\pm\frac{\epsilon}{2})}\eta-\zeta)|\right]
where we get smallness \epsilon R^{2}=\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} .
The compact support of \hat{W}_{1} and \hat{W}_{2} also indicates |\xi|\sim|\eta|\sim|\zeta| when |\xi|\gg M so that |\zeta|^{-\sigma} cancels |\xi|^{\sigma} from the \dot{H}^{\sigma} norm. Therefore, Young’s inequality implies
\eqref{eqinteract1}\lesssim_{d,b,a_{2},W_{1},W_{2}}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}.
For the high-frequency part, we again estimate pointwisely in the Fourier space. Let \tilde{f}=D^{\sigma}f and \tilde{t} to be either t\pm\frac{\epsilon}{2} . Compute
\displaystyle\mathscr{F}D^{\sigma}W_{2}D^{-\sigma}\left(1-\chi(R^{-1}D)\right)e^{i\tilde{t}\Delta_{b}}D^{\sigma}W_{1}D^{-\sigma}\tilde{f}(\xi)
(5.23)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle C|\xi|^{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{W}_{2}(\xi-\eta)(1-\chi(R^{-1}\eta))e^{b\tilde{t}\left(\sigma+\frac{d}{2}\right)}e^{-i\frac{e^{2b\tilde{t}}-1}{2b}|\eta|^{2}}\left(\widehat{W_{1}D^{-\sigma}\tilde{f}}\right)(e^{b\tilde{t}}\eta)d\eta
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-C\frac{be^{b\tilde{t}\left(\sigma+\frac{d}{2}\right)}}{e^{2b\tilde{t}}-1}|\xi|^{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\frac{e^{2b\tilde{t}}-1}{2b}|\eta|^{2}}
\displaystyle\qquad\nabla_{\eta}\cdot\left[\hat{W}_{2}(\xi-\eta)(1-\chi(R^{-1}\eta))\frac{\eta}{|\eta|^{2}}\hat{W}_{1}(e^{b\tilde{t}}\eta-\zeta)\right]|\zeta|^{-\sigma}\hat{\tilde{f}}(\zeta)d\zeta d\eta
where we integrate by parts over e^{-i\frac{e^{2b\tilde{t}}-1}{2b}|\eta|^{2}} . So from the support of 1-\chi(R^{-1}\eta) and \hat{V}_{1} , \hat{V}_{2} , we know |\xi|\sim|\zeta|\sim|\eta|\geq R . Hence by Young’s inequality,
\displaystyle|\eqref{eqfinitespeed}|\lesssim_{d,\sigma}R^{-1}\left|\frac{be^{b\tilde{t}\left(\sigma+1+\frac{d}{2}\right)}}{e^{2b\tilde{t}}-1}\right|\left\{(|\hat{W}_{2}|+|\nabla\hat{W}_{2}|)*\left[(|\hat{W}_{1}|+|\nabla\hat{W}_{1}|)*|\hat{\tilde{f}}|\right](e^{b\tilde{t}}\cdot)\right\}(\xi).
Young’s inequality again implies
\eqref{eqinteract2}\lesssim_{d,b,a_{1},a_{2},W_{1},W_{2}}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}.
So far, we have verified
\mathop{\rm sup}_{t\in[a_{1},a_{2}]}\eqref{eqinteract0}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}.
(2.b) The finite-rank part \tilde{V}_{2,2}:=-\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}P_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{H}}-i\mu) . We can assume \tilde{V}_{2,2}g=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(g,u_{i})_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}v_{i} with v_{i}\in\mathrm{Ran}P_{\rm disc} and u_{i}\in\mathrm{Ran}P_{\rm disc}^{*} . Since u_{i}\in\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}} and \langle x\rangle^{\gamma_{1}}v_{i}\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} (from Proposition 4.5 and (B.5)), we can approximate u_{i} with \tilde{u}_{i}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and obtain uniform smallness for t\in[a_{1},a_{2}] from the strongly continuity of the semigroup
\left(e^{i(t+\epsilon)({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}-e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}\right)^{*}\tilde{u}_{i}\to 0\quad\mathrm{in}\,\,\dot{H}^{\sigma}\,\,\mathrm{as}\,\,\epsilon\to 0.
So (5.19) and hence the second condition are confirmed by combining these two estimates for \tilde{V}_{2,1} and \tilde{V}_{2,2} .
(3) Recalling the definition of {\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-} in (4), we compute
\hat{A}(z)=I-i\int_{0}^{\infty}\tilde{V}_{2}e^{it({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}\tilde{V}_{1}e^{-itz}dt=I+\tilde{V}_{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{b}^{-}(z+ibs_{c})\tilde{V}_{1}.
The third follows Lemma 5.7 (2) directly.
Step 2. Proof of Strichartz estimate (1.29).
Now we define
U_{b}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)({\mathcal{H}}_{b}-ibs_{c})}ds.
The conclusion of Corollary 5.5 implies
\left(\int_{0}^{t}A(t-s)ds\right)^{-1}=\left(I-i\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}\tilde{V}_{1}\right)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t}([0,\infty),e^{b(\sigma-s_{c})t}\dot{H}^{\sigma}).
(5.24)
Also from the Strichartz estimate for e^{it\Delta_{b}} from (2.6) and the commutator relation (2.47),
we have
U_{b}\in\mathcal{L}\left(L_{t}^{q_{2}^{\prime}}e^{tb(\sigma-s_{c})}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}\to L_{t}^{q_{1}}e^{tb(\sigma-s_{c})}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}}\right)
(5.25)
where (q_{i},p_{i}) for i=1,2 satisfies the admissible condition (1.30).
Finally, we will come back to the modified system (5.6)-(5.7) and prove (1.29).
Viewing \tilde{V}\tilde{Z} as the source term and applying Duhamel’s formula, we get
\displaystyle\tilde{Z}=U_{b}\left[\delta_{t=0}\tilde{Z}(0)-i(-\tilde{V}\tilde{Z}+\tilde{F})\right].
Then we decompose \tilde{V}=\tilde{V}_{1}\tilde{V}_{2} according to Lemma 5.6, act \tilde{V}_{2} on both sides and invert I-i\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}\tilde{V}_{1} to get the formula for \tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{Z}
\tilde{V}_{2}\tilde{Z}=(I-i\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}\tilde{V}_{1})^{-1}\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}(\delta_{t=0}\tilde{Z}(0)-i\tilde{F}).
Plugging this representation into the previous formula for Z , we get
\tilde{Z}=\left[U_{b}+iU_{b}\tilde{V}_{1}(I-i\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}\tilde{V}_{1})^{-1}\tilde{V}_{2}U_{b}\right](\delta_{t=0}\tilde{Z}(0)-i\tilde{F})
(5.26)
The bounds (5.24), (5.25) and boundedness of \tilde{V}_{1} and \tilde{V}_{2} from Lemma 5.6 indicate that
\left\|e^{bt(\sigma-s_{c})}\tilde{Z}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{t}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{1}}_{x}}\lesssim\|\tilde{Z}(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}+\left\|e^{bt(\sigma-s_{c})}\tilde{F}\right\|_{L_{t}^{q_{2}^{\prime}}\dot{W}^{\sigma,p_{2}^{\prime}}_{x}}.
(5.27)
We emphasize that the enlarged admissible region of (5.24) and (5.25) is necessary because \tilde{V}_{1} and \tilde{V}_{2} can only slightly change the integrability.
Now consider Z with Z(0)=Z_{0}\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} satisfying the original system (1.28) and let \tilde{Z}(0)=P_{\rm ess}Z(0)\in\dot{H}^{\sigma} evolve in the modified system (5.6). Then we will have P_{\rm ess}Z(t)=\tilde{Z}(t) and the Strichartz estimate above implies (1.29) for \nu=b(\sigma-s_{c}) .
For general \nu\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) , it suffices to recover (5.24) and (5.25) with b(\sigma-s_{c}) substituted by \nu . Noticing that the condition of Corollary 5.5 for \epsilon:=\nu<b(\sigma-s_{c}) also holds, the above arguments also imply (5.24) for \nu . Since
\left\|e^{it\Delta_{b}}e^{-[b(\sigma-s_{c})-\nu]t}\right\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}\to L^{q}}\leq\left\|e^{it\Delta_{b}}\right\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}\to L^{q}}\lesssim|t|^{-\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{q}\right)},
this dispersive estimate similarly yields the free Strichartz (5.25) with exponential weight e^{\nu t} . These two elements conclude the proof of for all \nu\leq b(\sigma-s_{c}) .
∎
6. Nonlinear stability in \dot{H}^{\sigma}
In this section, we turn to the nonlinear stability theory self-similar blowup of (NLS) in \dot{H}^{\sigma} . More precisely, we will construct a finite-codimensional Lipschitz manifold of initial data leading to the self-similar blowup plus a decaying perturbation in \dot{H}^{\sigma} , and show that this implies the asymptotic stability assuming mode stability Assumption 1.2.
We begin by clarifying our setting. Recall (NLS) under the self-similar renormalization (1.3) with u=Q_{b}+\varepsilon , Z=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon\\
{\overline{\varepsilon}}\end{array}\right) as
i\partial_{\tau}Z+\mathcal{H}Z=F(Z).
(1.21)
where {\mathcal{H}} is the linear operator (1.4) with potential (1.26), Z=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Z_{1}\\
Z_{2}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2} and F=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-R(Z_{1},Q_{b})\\
\overline{R({\overline{Z}_{2}},Q_{b})}\end{array}\right) with
\displaystyle R(\varepsilon,v)=(\varepsilon+v)|\varepsilon+v|^{p-1}-v|v|^{p-1}-\frac{p+1}{2}|v|^{p-1}\varepsilon-\frac{p-1}{2}v^{2}|v|^{p-3}{\overline{\varepsilon}}.
(6.1)
From the decay of self-similar profile (1.7), the potential satisfies (1.27) with \alpha=2 . Therefore, the exponent from (4.19) now reads as
\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{0}:=\frac{1}{16}\min\left\{\frac{d}{2}-s_{c},1-s_{c}\right\}>0,\\
p_{0}:=\left(\max\left\{\frac{d-2}{2d},\frac{\sigma}{d}\right\}+\frac{\delta_{0}s_{c}}{d^{2}(p+1)}\right)^{-1}\in\left(2,\frac{2d}{d-2}\right).\end{array}\right.
(6.2)
6.1. \mathcal{J} -invariance and spectrally decoupled system
In this subsection, we will transform the nonlinear Schrödinger equation into a renormalized system with stable modes and unstable modes decoupled. The extra degree of freedom in the system will be removed by an \mathbb{R} -linear operator {\mathcal{J}} (borrowed from [60]).
{\mathcal{J}} -invariance. We define the \mathbb{R} -linear subspace
X_{+}:=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon\\
{\overline{\varepsilon}}\end{array}\right):\varepsilon\in\dot{H}^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}\subset\dot{H}^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\dot{H}^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(6.3)
and the \mathbb{R} -linear transform on (\dot{H}^{\sigma})^{2}
{\mathcal{J}}:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Z_{1}\\
Z_{2}\end{array}\right)\mapsto\overline{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Z_{2}\\
Z_{1}\end{array}\right)}
(6.4)
Obviously, for Z\in(\dot{H}^{\sigma})^{2} , Z\in X_{+} if and only if {\mathcal{J}}Z=Z . With the help of {\mathcal{J}} , we can show X_{+} is invariant under the evolution (1.21).
Lemma 6.1 ( {\mathcal{J}} -invariance for (1.21)).
We have
